Such division is expected to be nontrivial for most real-world cases.

Chapter 4
Policy Improvement Bounds for Markov Decision Processes

Policy improvement bounds on the difference of the discounted and average returns play a crucial role in the theoretical justification of the trust-region policy optimization (TRPO), proximal policy optimization (PPO), and related algorithms. Theorem
1
in Section
2.2.3
is an example of a policy improvement bound.

In this chapter we focus our investigation on policy improvement bounds due to discrepancy between the implication of the existing bounds in the literature and common practical use of reinforcement learning algorithms. The policy improvement bound in
[
118
,
4
]
leads to a degenerate bound when the discount factor approaches one, making the applicability of TRPO and related algorithms questionable when the discount factor is close to one. We refine the results in
[
118
,
4
]
and propose a novel bound that is continuous in the discount factor. Furthermore, we generalize the obtained novel bounds on Markov decision process (MDP) problems with countable state spaces and on semi-Markov decision process (SMDP) problems.

In
[
64
]
the authors developed a conservative
policy iteration algorithm for MDPs
that can avoid catastrophic large policy updates; each iteration
generates a new policy as a mixture of the old policy and a greedy
policy. They proved that the updated policy is guaranteed to
improve when the greedy policy is properly chosen and the updated
policy is sufficiently close to the old one. In

[
118
]
the authors generalized the proof of

[
64
]
to a policy improvement bound for two

arbitrary
randomized policies. This policy improvement
bound allows one to find an updated policy that guarantees to
improve by solving an unconstrained optimization problem.

[
118
]
also proposed a practical algorithm, called
trust region policy optimization (TRPO), that approximates the
theoretically-justified update scheme by solving a constrained
optimization problem in each iteration. In recent years, several
modifications of TRPO have been proposed
[
119
,
117
,
4
,
3
]
. These studies continued
to exploit the policy improvement bound to theoretically motivate
their algorithms.

The policy improvement bounds in
[
118
,
4
]
are lower bounds on the difference of the expected

discounted reward returns
under two policies. Unfortunately, the
use of these policy improvement bounds becomes questionable and
inconclusive when the discount factor is close to one. These policy
improvement bounds degenerate as discount factor converges to one.
That is, the lower bounds on the difference of discounted reward returns
converge to negative infinity as the discount factor goes to one,
although the difference of discounted returns converges to the
difference of (finite) average rewards. Nevertheless, numerical experiments demonstrate that the TRPO
algorithm and its variations perform best when the discount factor

\gamma
is close to one, a region that the existing
bounds do not justify; e.g.
[
118
,
119
,
117
]
used
\gamma=0.99
, and
[
119
,
4
]
used
\gamma=0.995
in their experiments.

Recent studies
[
141
]
and
[
32
]
(see Section
2.2.3
)
proposed policy improvement bounds for average returns, justifying that
a family of TRPO algorithms can be used for continuing problems with
long-run average reward or cost objectives. It remains unclear how the
large values of the discount factor can be justified and why the
policy improvement bounds in
[
118
,
4
]
for the
discounted returns do not converge to one of the bounds provided in

[
32
,
141
]
.

In this chapter we conduct a comprehensive study on policy improvement bounds in different settings, i.e. for average and discounted cost objectives, finite and countable state spaces, MDP and SMDP models. Following the narrative of Chapter
2
we focus on cost minimization MDP and SMDP problems. As a result, unlike some other papers
[
118
,
4
,
141
]
that proposed lower bounds on the difference of the expected reward returns, we derive
upper bounds
on the difference of the expected cost returns.

We summarize the major contributions of this chapter:

1.

In Section
4.1
we provide a unified derivation of policy
improvement bounds for both discounted and average cost MDPs on finite state spaces. Our
bounds depend on the discount factor
continuously
. When the
discount factor converges to
1
, the corresponding
bound for discounted returns converges to a policy improvement
bound for average costs.
Our results justify the use of a large discount
factor in TRPO algorithm and its variations.

2.

Policy improvement bounds proposed for finite state spaces in Section
4.1
are not valid for MDPs on infinite state spaces. In Section
4.2
we obtain policy improvement bounds on the difference of performances of two policies of an MDP on the countable state space,
assuming
\mathcal{V}
-uniform ergodicity of the transition matrix of one of the policies. We introduce a
\mathcal{V}
-weighted ergodicity coefficient and relate it to the
\mathcal{V}
-uniform ergodicity assumption.

3.

In Section
4.3
we provide performance identity and policy improvement bound for an SMDP. These results imply that TRPO, PPO, and related algorithms can be applied for SMDPs.

Section
4.1
is based on
[
31
]
. The results in Sections
4.2
and
4.3
have not been published elsewhere before.

4.1
Policy improvement bounds in finite state spaces

In this section we provide a single policy improvement bound for
both discounted and average cost objectives for an MDP on a finite state space.
This result is achieved by two innovative
observations. First, we embed the discounted future state
distribution under a fixed policy as the stationary distribution of
a modified Markov chain. Second, we introduce an

ergodicity coefficient
from Markov chain perturbation theory
to bound the one-norm of the difference of discounted future state
distributions, and prove that this bound is optimal in a certain
sense.

4.1.1
Preliminaries

We consider an MDP defined by the tuple
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A},P,g,\mu)
, where
\mathcal{X}
is a finite state space;
\mathcal{A}
is a finite action space;
P(y|x,a)
is the probability of transitioning into state
y
upon taking action
a
in state
x
;

g:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the cost function;
\mu
is the probability distribution of the initial state
x^{(0)}
.

We let
\pi
denote a stationary randomized policy
\pi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{A})
, where
\Delta(\mathcal{A})
is the probability simplex over
\mathcal{A}
. Under policy
\pi
, the corresponding Markov chain has a transition matrix
P_{\pi}
given by

P_{\pi}(x,y):=\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a|x)P(y|x,a),~x,y\in\mathcal{X}.

We assume that MDPs we consider are unichain, meaning that for any stationary policy
\pi
the corresponding Markov chain with transition matrix
P_{\pi}
contains only one recurrent class
[
109
]
.
We use
d_{\pi}
to denote a unique stationary distribution of a Markov chain with transition matrix
P_{\pi}
.

We discuss two formulations of an MDP problem: with infinite horizon discounted cost objective and long-run average cost objective.

We let
\gamma\in[0,1)
be a discount factor. We define the value function for a given policy
\pi
as

V^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}g(x^{(t)},a^{(t)})~\Big|~x^{(0)}=x\right],

where
x^{(t)}
,
a^{(t)}
are random variables for the state and action at time
t
upon executing the policy
\pi
from the initial state
x
. For policy
\pi
we define the state-action value function as

Q^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}(x,a):=g(x,a)+\gamma\underset{y\sim P_{\pi}(\cdot|x,a)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[V_{\pi}^{(\gamma)}(y)\right],

and the advantage function as

A^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}(x,a):=Q^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}(x,a)-V^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}(x).

We define the discounted future state distribution of policy
\pi
as

\displaystyle d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}(x):=(1-\gamma)\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\mathbb{P}\Big[x^{(t)}=x~|~x^{(0)}\sim\mu;x^{(1)},x^{(2)},...\sim\pi\Big].

We measure the performance of policy
\pi
by its expected discounted return from the initial state distribution
\mu
:

\displaystyle\eta^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}(\mu):=(1-\gamma)\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}\left[V^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}(x)\right]=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}\\
a\sim\pi(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}[g(x,a)].

In the following lemma we give an alternative definition of the discounted future state distribution as a stationary distribution of a modified transition matrix. See Appendix
C.1
for the proof of the following Lemma
10
.

Lemma 10
.

For a stationary policy
\pi
, we define a discounted transition matrix for policy
\pi
as

\displaystyle P_{\pi}^{(\gamma)}:=\gamma P_{\pi}+(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T},

(4.1.1)

where
e:=(1,1,\dotsc,1)^{T}
is a vector of ones,
e\mu^{T}
is the matrix which rows are equal to
\mu^{T}
.

Then the discounted future state distribution of policy
\pi
,
d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}
, is the stationary distribution of transition matrix
P_{\pi}^{(\gamma)}
.

The long-run average cost of policy
\pi
is defined as

\displaystyle\eta_{\pi}:

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum\limits_{t=0}^{N-1}g(x^{(t)},a^{(t)})~|~x^{(0)}\sim\mu\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d_{\pi}\\
a\sim\pi(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[g(x,a)\right].

The long-run average cost
\eta_{\pi}
does not depend on the initial state initial state distribution
\mu
, since
the Markov chain with transition matrix
P_{\pi}
is assumed to be a unichain, see
[
109
, Section 8.2]
.

For an MDP with a long-run average cost objective we define the relative value function

\displaystyle V_{\pi}(x):=\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum\limits_{t=0}^{N-1}\left(g(x^{(t)},a^{(t)})-\eta_{\pi}\right)~|~x^{(0)}=x\right],

the relative state-action value function
Q_{\pi}(x,a):=g(x,a)-\eta_{\pi}+\mathbb{E}_{y\sim P_{\pi}(\cdot|x,a)}\left[V_{\pi}(y)\right],
and the relative advantage function
A_{\pi}(x,a):=Q_{\pi}(x,a)-V_{\pi}(x)
. The following relations hold for value, state-action value, and advantage functions.

Lemma 11
.

We let
\pi
be a stationary policy,
\gamma
be the discount factor, and
\mu
be the initial state distribution. Then the following limits hold for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
:

\displaystyle\eta_{\pi}

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{\gamma\rightarrow 1}\eta_{\pi}^{(\gamma)}

(4.1.2)

\displaystyle V_{\pi}(x)

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{\gamma\rightarrow 1}\left(V_{\pi}^{(\gamma)}(x)-(1-\gamma)^{-1}\eta_{\pi}\right),

(4.1.3)

\displaystyle Q_{\pi}(x,a)

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{\gamma\rightarrow 1}\left(Q_{\pi}^{(\gamma)}(x,a)-(1-\gamma)^{-1}\eta_{\pi}\right),

\displaystyle A_{\pi}(x,a)

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{\gamma\rightarrow 1}A_{\pi}^{(\gamma)}(x,a).

The proofs of identities (
4.1.2
), (
4.1.3
) can be found in
[
109
, Section 8]
. The rest results of Lemma
11
follow directly.

4.1.2
Novel policy improvement bounds

The policy improvement bound in
[
118
,
4
]
for the discounted returns serves to theoretically justify the TRPO algorithm and its variations.
The following lemma is a reproduction of Corollary 1 in
[
4
]
. We state its upper bound version because it is more appropriate for MDPs with cost minimization objectives.

Lemma 12

(Corollary 1 in
[
4
]
)
.

For any two policies
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
the following bound holds:

\eta^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}(\mu)-\eta^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}(\mu)\leq\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}},a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]+\frac{2\gamma\epsilon^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}}{1-\gamma}\underset{x\sim d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\text{TV}\Big(\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)~||~\pi_{1}(\cdot|x)\Big)\right],

(4.1.4)

where
{\text{TV}}\Big(\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)||\pi_{1}(\cdot|x)\Big):=\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\lvert\pi_{2}(a|x)-\pi_{1}(a|x)\rvert
, and
\epsilon^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}:=\max\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Big|\underset{a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)}{\mathbb{E}}[A^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)]\Big|
.

We provide a summary of the proof of
[
4
, Corollary 1]
in Appendix
C.1
.

The left-hand side of (
4.1.4
) converges to the difference of long-run average costs as
\gamma\rightarrow 1
. Unfortunately, the right-hand side of (
4.1.4
) converges to the positive infinity because of
(1-\gamma)^{-1}
factor in the second term.
Our goal is to get a new policy improvement bound for discounted returns that does not degenerate.

The group inverse
D
of a matrix
A
is the unique matrix such that

ADA=A,~DAD=D,\text{ and }DA=AD.
From
[
90
]
, we know that if stochastic matrix
P
is aperiodic and irreducible then the group inverse matrix of
I-P
is well-defined and equals to

D=\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}(P^{t}-ed^{T}),
where
d
is the stationary distribution of
P
.
[
90
]
also established a connection between the fundamental matrix of transition matrix
P
and the group inverse
D
of a matrix
P
:

\displaystyle D=Z-ed^{T},

(4.1.5)

where
Z:=\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}\left(P-ed^{T}\right)^{t}
is called the fundamental matrix of
P
, see
[
68
]
.

We let
D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}
be the group inverse of matrix
I-P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}
, where
P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}
is defined by (
4.1.1
).
Following
[
120
]
, we define a one-norm ergodicity coefficient for a matrix
A
as

\displaystyle\tau_{1}[A]:=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\|x\|_{1}=1\\
x^{T}e=0\end{subarray}}{\max}\|A^{T}x\|_{1}.

(4.1.6)

The one-norm ergodicity coefficient has important property that

\tau_{1}[A]=\tau_{1}[A+ec^{T}],~\text{for any vector }c.

(4.1.7)

By Lemma
13
below,
\tau_{1}\left[D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}\right]=\tau_{1}\left[(I-\gamma P_{\pi})^{-1}\right]
, for
\gamma<1
.

Lemma 13
.

We let
\pi
be an arbitrary policy. Then

\displaystyle D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}=(I-\gamma P_{\pi})^{-1}+e(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}(I-\left(I-\gamma P_{\pi}\right)^{-1})-ed_{\pi}^{T}.

The proof of Lemma
13
can be found in Appendix
C.1
.

We are ready to state the main result of Section
4.1
.

Theorem 3
.

The following bound on the difference of discounted returns of two policies
{\pi_{1}}
and
\pi_{2}
holds:

\displaystyle\eta^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}(\mu)-\eta^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}(\mu)\leq\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}\\
a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]+2\gamma\epsilon^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}\tau_{1}\left[D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}\right]\underset{x\sim d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\text{TV}\Big(\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)~||~\pi_{1}(\cdot|x)\Big)\right].

(4.1.8)

Proof of Theorem
3
.

We closely follow the first steps in the proof of Lemma 2 in
[
4
]
and start with

\displaystyle\eta^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}(\mu)-\eta^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}(\mu)\leq\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}\\
a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]+\max\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Big|\underset{a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)}{\mathbb{E}}[A^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)]\Big|~\left\|d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}-d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}\right\|_{1}.

Next, unlike
[
4
]
, we obtain an upper bound on
\|d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}-d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}\|_{1}
that does not degenerate as
\gamma\rightarrow 1
. We use the following perturbation identity:

\displaystyle(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}})^{T}-(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}})^{T}

\displaystyle=(D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}})^{T}(P_{\pi_{1}}^{(\gamma)}-P_{\pi_{2}}^{(\gamma)})d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}

(4.1.9)

\displaystyle=\gamma(D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}})^{T}(P_{\pi_{1}}-P_{\pi_{2}})d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}.

Identity (
4.1.9
) follows from the perturbation identity for stationary distributions, see equation (4.1) in
[
91
]
, and the fact that
d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}
and
d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}
are the stationary distributions of the discounted transition matrices
P_{\pi_{2}}^{(\gamma)}
and
P_{\pi_{1}}^{(\gamma)}
, respectively.
We make use of the ergodicity coefficient (
4.1.6
) to get a new perturbation bound:

\displaystyle\|d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}-d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\gamma\left\|\left(D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}\right)^{T}(P_{\pi_{1}}-P_{\pi_{2}})^{T}d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\tau_{1}\left[D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}\right]\left\|(P_{\pi_{1}}-P_{\pi_{2}})^{T}d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}\right\|_{1}

(4.1.10)

\displaystyle\leq 2\gamma\tau_{1}\left[D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}\right]\underset{x\sim d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\text{TV}\Big(\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)~||~\pi_{1}(\cdot|x)\Big)\right],

where first equality follows from (
4.1.9
), first inequality follows from ergodicity coefficient (
4.1.6
) definition and the fact that
\left((P_{\pi_{1}}-P_{\pi_{2}})^{T}d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}\right)^{T}e=0
, the second inequality follows from a definition of the total variation distance, see the proof of
[
4
, Lemma 3]
.
∎

The novel policy improvement bound (
4.1.8
) converges to a meaningful bound on the difference of average costs as
\gamma
goes to 1. Corollary
1
follows from Theorem
3
, Lemma
11
and the fact that
\tau_{1}\left[D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}\right]\rightarrow\tau_{1}\left[D_{\pi_{2}}\right]
as
\gamma\rightarrow 1
.

Corollary 1
.

The following bound on the difference of long-run average costs of two policies
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
holds:

\displaystyle\eta_{\pi_{2}}-\eta_{\pi_{1}}\leq\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d_{\pi_{1}}\\
a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]+2\epsilon_{\pi_{2}}\tau_{1}\left[D_{\pi_{2}}\right]\underset{x\sim d_{\pi_{1}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\text{TV}\Big(\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)~||~\pi_{1}(\cdot|x)\Big)\right],

(4.1.11)

where
D_{\pi_{2}}
is the group inverse of matrix
I-P_{\pi_{2}}
,
\epsilon_{\pi_{2}}:=\max\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Big|\underset{a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)}{\mathbb{E}}[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)]\Big|
.

4.1.3
Interpretation of
\tau_{1}\left[D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}\right]

We provide several bounds on
\tau_{1}[D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}]
to reveal its dependency on the discount factor
\gamma
and policy
\pi
.
First, we compute
\tau_{1}[D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}]
using geometric convergence rates of transition matrices to their stationary distributions.
For any policy
\pi
, there exists constants
k_{\pi}\in(0,1)
and
C_{\pi}>0
such that
\|P_{\pi}^{t}(x,\cdot)-d_{\pi}(\cdot)\|_{1}\leq C_{\pi}k_{\pi}^{t},
for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
, where
d_{\pi}
is the stationary distribution of transition matrix
P_{\pi}
.
By Appendix Section 5 in
[
29
]
,
k_{\pi}
can be taken to be
|\lambda_{2}|
, the largest number among the absolute values of the
eigenvalues of
P_{\pi}
excluding the eigenvalue 1.

Matrix
P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}
defined by (
4.1.1
) is called the Google matrix, see
[
76
]
. If the spectrum of transition matrix
P_{\pi}
is
\{1,\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},...,\lambda_{n}\}
, then the spectrum of matrix
P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}
is
\{1,\gamma\lambda_{2},\gamma\lambda_{3},...,\gamma\lambda_{n}\}
.
Google matrix
P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}
exhibits a faster convergence rate than
P_{\pi}
: there exists constant
C^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}>0
, such that
\|(P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{t}(x,\cdot)-d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}(\cdot)\|_{1}\leq C_{\pi}^{(\gamma)}|\gamma\lambda_{2}|^{t},\text{ for each }x\in\mathcal{X}.
Inequalities
\tau_{1}\left[D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}\right]\leq\left\|D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}\left\|(P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{t}-e(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}\right\|_{\infty}
lead to the following bound.

Lemma 14
.

We let
D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}
be the group inverse matrix of
I-P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}
. Then for any discount factor
\gamma\in(0,1]
there exists
C_{\pi}^{(\gamma)}>0
such that

\displaystyle\tau_{1}\left[d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}\right]\leq C_{\pi}^{(\gamma)}\frac{1}{1-\gamma|\lambda_{2}|},

where
\lambda_{2}
is an eigenvalue of
P_{\pi}
with the second largest absolute value.

It may be difficult to express how constant
C_{\pi}^{(\gamma)}
depends on the discount factor for a general transition matrix
P_{\pi}.
In Lemma
15
below we derive another upper bound on
\tau_{1}\left[D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}\right]
that does not include additional constants dependent on
\gamma
. The proof of Lemma
15
is in Appendix
C.1
.

For a given policy
\pi
, we assume the transition matrix
P_{\pi}
is aperiodic and irreducible. By Proposition 1.7 in
[
78
]
, there exists an integer
\ell
such that
P_{\pi}^{q}(x,y)>0
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
, and
q\geq\ell.
Then, there exists a sufficiently small constant
\delta_{\pi}^{(\mu)}>0
, such that

\displaystyle P_{\pi}^{\ell}(x,y)\geq\delta^{(\mu)}_{\pi}\mu(y),\quad\text{ for each }x,y\in\mathcal{X},

(4.1.12)

where
\mu
denotes the distribution of the initial state.

Lemma 15
.

We let
D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}
be the group inverse matrix of
I-P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}
.

We let
\delta^{(\mu)}_{\pi}
be a constant that satisfies (
4.1.12
) for
P_{\pi}
and some integer
\ell
. Then

\displaystyle\tau_{1}[D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}]\leq\frac{2\ell}{1-\gamma+\gamma^{\ell}\delta_{\pi}^{(\mu)}},

where
\delta^{(\mu)}_{\pi}
and
\ell
are independent of
\gamma
.

4.1.4
Condition numbers in policy improvement bounds

Lemma
16
demonstrates that inequality (
4.1.2
) is the best (smallest) norm-wise bound on the difference of stationary distributions given ”averaged” transition matrix perturbation.
Lemma
16
directly follows from the results in
[
71
]
.

Lemma 16
.

We consider an irreducible and aperiodic transition matrix
P
with the stationary distribution
d
. We say that
\tau[P]
is a condition number of matrix
P
if inequality

\displaystyle\|d-\tilde{d}\|_{1}\leq\tau[P]~\|(\tilde{P}-P)^{T}\tilde{d}\|_{1},

(4.1.13)

holds for any irreducible and aperiodic transition matrix
\tilde{P}
with the stationary distribution
\tilde{d}
.
We let
D
be a group inverse matrix of
I-P
.

Then
\tau_{1}[D]
is the smallest condition number, i.e. inequality
\tau_{1}[D]\leq\tau[P]

holds for any condition number
\tau[P]
satisfying (
4.1.13
).

Lemma
16
shows that inequality (
4.1.2
) in the proof of Theorem
1
is a key to the improvement of the policy improvement bounds in
[
118
,
4
]
. Moreover, it follows from Lemma
16
that Corollary
1
provides a better policy improvement bound for the average cost criterion than
[
32
,
141
]
. We note that in Section
2.2.3
we essentially used
\|Z\|_{\mathcal{V}}
, where
Z
is the fundamental matrix, as a condition number. In finite state spaces this condition number corresponds to
\|Z\|_{\infty}
, i.e.
\mathcal{V}\equiv 1
. In contrast,
[
141
]
used Kemeny’s constant (
4.1.14
) as a condition number. Lemma
17
compares these condition numbers with
\tau_{1}[D]
. The proof of Lemma
17
can be found in Appendix
C.1
.

Lemma 17
.

We consider an irreducible and aperiodic transition matrix
P
. We let
M:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
be the mean first hitting time matrix, where
M(x,y)
is the expected number of steps is taken to reach state
y
from state
x
for the Markov chain with transition matrix
P
, for each
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
. We note
M(x,x)=0
, for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
. We let
d
be the stationary distribution of
P
.

We define Kemeny’s constant of transition matrix
P
as

\kappa:=\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}d(y)M(x,y),

(4.1.14)

where
\kappa
is a constant independent of
x\in\mathcal{X}
, see
[
68
]
.
Matrices
D
and
Z
are the group inverse of
I-P
and the fundamental matrix of
P
, respectively.

Then

(a)

\tau_{1}[D]=\tau_{1}[Z]=\tau_{1}[MI_{d}]
;

(b)

\tau_{1}[D]\leq\|Z\|_{\infty}
;

(c)

\tau_{1}[D]=\kappa-\min\limits_{x,y\in\mathcal{X}}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathcal{X}}d(z)\min[M(x,z),M(y,z)]\leq\kappa
,

where
I_{d}
is the diagonal matrix with diagonal elements
d(x)
,
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

There is no general superior relation between
\|Z\|_{\infty}
and
\kappa
. In other words, either
\|Z\|_{\infty}
or
\kappa
may provide a superior bound for different examples of transition matrices, see
[
59
]
.

4.2
Policy improvement bounds for countable state spaces

In this section we derive proper policy improvement bounds for MDPs on countable state spaces. As we observed in Section
2.2.3
, a policy improvement bound is a necessary building block if we want to design a deep RL algorithm for MDP problems with countable state spaces. In this section we provide more detailed exploration of this topic. We discuss degeneracy of the bounds derived in Section
4.1
for countable state spaces, propose novel policy improvement bounds via a
\mathcal{V}
-weighted one-norm ergodicity coefficient, and provide bounds on this ergodicity coefficient via the Lyapunov function
\mathcal{V}
.

4.2.1
Preliminaries

Policy improvement bounds obtained in Section
4.1
are not valid in countable state spaces for most MDPs with long-run average cost objectives. These bounds have been refined by incorporating sharp perturbation bounds on the difference between stationary distributions. These perturbation bounds depend on condition numbers that typically become infinite for DTMC on countable state spaces. Specifically, Kemeny’s constant
\kappa
is infinite for any DTMC on countable state spaces
[
6
,
80
]
, whereas
\|Z\|_{\infty}
and
\tau_{1}[D]
are guaranteed to be finite only for uniformly ergodic Markov chains
[
94
, Section 13]
,
[
79
]
.

In this section we consider an MDP problem with a countable state space
\mathcal{X}
, finite action space
\mathcal{A}
, one-step cost function
g(x,a)
, transition function
P(\cdot|x,a)
, and long-run average cost objective:

\displaystyle\eta_{\pi}:=\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum\limits_{t=0}^{N-1}g(x^{(t)},a^{(t)})~|~x^{(0)}\sim\mu\right],

(4.2.1)

where
\mu
is an initial state distribution. We note that one-step cost function
g
might be unbounded. Existence of a Lyapunov function
\mathcal{V}
such that
|g|\leq\mathcal{V}
is a sufficient condition for the long-run average cost (
4.2.1
) to be finite, see Lemma
1
in Section
2.2.1
.

We recall that policy
\pi
and its corresponding transition matrix
P_{\pi}
satisfy the drift condition if there exists a Lyapunov function
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[1,\infty)
, constants
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
b\geq 0
, and a finite subset
C\subset\mathcal{X}
such that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P_{\pi}(y|x)\mathcal{V}(y)\leq\varepsilon\mathcal{V}(x)+b\mathbb{I}_{C}(x),\quad\text{for each }x\in\mathcal{X},

(4.2.2)

where
\mathbb{I}_{C}(x)=1
if
x\in C
and
\mathbb{I}_{C}(x)=0
otherwise.

4.2.2
Novel policy improvement bound

Following
[
66
, Chapter 2]
, we define a
\mathcal{V}
-weighted one-norm ergodicity coefficient for a
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
matrix
A
and function
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[1,\infty)
as

\displaystyle\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}[A]:

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\|x\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}=1\\
x^{T}e=0\end{subarray}}{\sup}\|A^{T}x\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{x,y\in\mathcal{X}}\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}(x)+\mathcal{V}(y)}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathcal{X}}|A(x,z)-A(y,z)|\mathcal{V}(z),

(4.2.3)

where
\|\nu\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}:=\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|\nu(x)|\mathcal{V}(x)
for any
\nu:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
A(x,y)
is the
(x,y)
th element of matrix
A
for
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
.

Definition (
4.2.2
) directly implies that the following property continues to hold for the
\mathcal{V}
-weighted one-norm ergodicity coefficient:

\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}[A]=\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}[A+ec^{T}],~\text{for any vector }c,

(4.2.4)

for any
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[1,\infty).

We state our policy improvement bound for an MDP on countable state space with long-run average cost objective.

Theorem 4
.

We consider two policies
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
. We assume that transition matrix
P_{\pi_{2}}
of policy
\pi_{2}
is such that the drift condition (
4.2.2
) holds for a Lyapunov function
\mathcal{V}\geq 1
and the cost function satisfies
|g|\leq\mathcal{V}
. We also assume that transition matrix
P_{\pi_{1}}
of policy
\pi_{1}
is positive recurrent and has stationary distribution
d_{\pi_{1}}
.

Then ergodicity coefficient
\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}\left[D_{\pi_{2}}\right]
is finite and the following bound on the difference of average returns of two policies
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
holds:

\displaystyle\eta_{\pi_{2}}-\eta_{\pi_{1}}\leq\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d_{\pi_{1}}\\
a\sim\pi_{1}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{\pi_{2}(a|x)}{\pi_{1}(a|x)}A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]+2\epsilon_{\pi_{2},\mathcal{V}}\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}\left[D_{\pi_{2}}\right]\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d_{\pi_{1}}\\
a\sim\pi_{1}(\cdot|x)\\
y\sim P(\cdot|x,a)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left|\frac{\pi_{2}(a|x)}{\pi_{1}(a|x)}-1\right|\mathcal{V}(y)\right].

(4.2.5)

where
\epsilon_{\pi_{2},\mathcal{V}}:=\left\|\underset{a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)}{\mathbb{E}}[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)]\right\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}.

The proof of Theorem
4
is provided in Appendix
C.2
.

Remark 6
.

In practice, when a deep reinforcement learning algorithm is designed, policy
\pi_{1}
is interpreted as a current policy and policy
\pi_{2}
as a next next. In other words, policy
\pi_{2}
is interpreted as an unknown policy that is yet to be found, see, for example, Section
2.2.4
. Therefore, we need to make additional assumptions to ensure that next policy
\pi_{2}
satisfies the drift condition with some known Lyapunov function. Some authors, for example
[
56
]
, make a strong assumption that all policy of an MDP under consideration satisfy the drift condition for same Lyapunov function
\mathcal{V}
, i.e. all policies are
\mathcal{V}
-uniform ergodic. In Chapter
2
we developed another approach. We assumed that current policy
\pi_{1}
satisfies the drift condition and the change from policy
\pi_{1}
to policy
\pi_{2}
is sufficiently small to preserve
\mathcal{V}
-uniform ergodicity, see Theorem
1
. Several papers proposed other sufficient conditions to ensure that a transition matrix continues to be
\mathcal{V}
-uniform ergodic after update or perturbation, see
[
42
,
113
,
40
,
57
,
99
]
.

4.2.3
Interpretation of
\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}\left[D\right]

We consider a Markov chain on countable state space such that its corresponding transition matrix
P
satisfies the drift condition (
4.2.2
). In this section we relate ergodicity coefficient
\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}\left[D\right]
to the drift condition, where
D
is the group inverse of
I-P
. We provide several bounds on
\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}\left[D\right]
in terms of Lyapunov function
\mathcal{V}
, constants
\varepsilon
and
b
, finite set
C
used in the drift condition.

We start with a simple case when finite set
C
consists of a single state
C=\{x^{*}\}
. Similar bounds were proposed in
[
79
, Corollary 3.1.]
. We provide the proof of Lemma
18
in Appendix
C.2
.

Lemma 18
.

We assume that transition matrix
P
satisfies the following drift condition:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(y|x)\mathcal{V}(y)\leq\varepsilon\mathcal{V}(x)+b\mathbb{I}_{x=x^{*}}(x),\quad\text{for each }x\in\mathcal{X},

(4.2.6)

where
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[1,\infty)
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
b\geq 0
.

Then

\displaystyle\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}[D]\leq\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}\left(1+\min\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}[\mathcal{V}(x)]~\|d\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}\right),

where
d
is the stationary distribution of
P
. Norm
\|d\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}
can be further bounded as

\displaystyle\|d\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{b}{1-\varepsilon}d(x^{*}).

(4.2.7)

We provide an example of applying Lemma
18
.

Example 1
.

We consider the Bernoulli random walk on the integer lattice
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
with transition probabilities
P(x,x+1)=\lambda
for
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
P(x,x-1)=\mu
for
x\geq 1
, and
P(0,0)=\mu
. We assume that
\lambda+\mu=1
and
\rho:=\frac{\lambda}{\mu}<1
.

This Markov chain satisfies the following drift condition with
\mathcal{V}(x)=\rho^{-x/2}
,
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and
C=\{0\}
:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}P(x,y)\mathcal{V}(y)\leq\frac{2\sqrt{\rho}}{1+\rho}\mathcal{V}(x)+\frac{1-\sqrt{\rho}}{1+\rho}I_{x=0}(x),\text{ for each }x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

(4.2.8)

We provide the proof that the Bernoulli random walk transition probabilities satisfy (
4.2.8
) in Appendix
C.2
. Lyapunov function
\mathcal{V}(x)=\rho^{-x/2}
was proposed in
[
82
]
.

It is known that the stationary distribution of the Bernoulli random walk is
d(x)=(1-\rho)\rho^{x}
for
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.
Hence, we can explicitly find

\displaystyle\|d\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=1+\sqrt{\rho}.

From Lemma
18
, we get that

\displaystyle\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}[D]\leq\frac{1+\rho}{(1-\sqrt{\rho})^{2}}\left(2+\sqrt{\rho}\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{(1-\rho)^{2}}\right),

where
D
is the group inverse matrix of
I-P
.
We should mention that same order bound on
\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}[D]
for the Bernoulli random walk was previously obtained in
[
98
]
.

Next, we consider a more general case where finite state
C
might include more than one state. The proof of Lemma
19
can be found in Appendix
C.2
.

Lemma 19
.

We assume that transition matrix
P
satisfies the drift condition (
4.2.2
) for Lyapunov function
\mathcal{V}
, constants
\varepsilon,b
, and finite set
C
such that

\displaystyle b\leq\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(x^{*},y)\mathcal{V}(y),

(4.2.9)

where
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
is such that
\sum\limits_{x\in C}P(x^{*},x)>0
.

Then

\displaystyle\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}[D]\leq\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}+\min\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}[\mathcal{V}(x)]\frac{b}{(1-\varepsilon)^{2}}\sum\limits_{x\in C}d(x).

The discounting can be considered as an update or perturbation of the initial Markov chain. Lemma
19
allows to bound the ergodicity coefficient of
D^{(\gamma)}
, where
D^{(\gamma)}
is the group inverse matrix of
I-P^{(\gamma)}
, if the drift condition holds for transition matrix
P
. We recall definition of a discounted transition matrix:

\displaystyle P^{(\gamma)}:=\gamma P+(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T},

where
\mu
is a state distribution,
\gamma\in(0,1]
is a discount factor.

We start with Lemma
20
that shows that the discounted transition matrix
P^{(\gamma)}
is
\mathcal{V}
-uniformly ergodic if initial transition matrix
P
satisfies the drift condition for Lyapunov function
\mathcal{V}
. The proof of Lemma
20
can be found in Appendix
C.2
.

Lemma 20
.

We assume that transition matrix
P
satisfies the drift condition (
4.2.2
).

Then the modified transition matrix
P^{(\gamma)}:=\gamma P+(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T}
satisfies the following drift condition

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P^{(\gamma)}(x,y)\mathcal{V}(y)\leq\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon\gamma+1)\mathcal{V}(x)+\max[\gamma b,(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\mathcal{V}]\mathbb{I}_{C\cup\Omega}(x),

(4.2.10)

where
\Omega=\left\{x\in\mathcal{X}:\mathcal{V}(x)<\frac{2(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\mathcal{V}}{1-\gamma\varepsilon}\right\}
,
\gamma\in(0,1]
.

Corollary
2
directly follows from Lemma
19
and Lemma
20
.

Corollary 2
.

We assume that transition matrix
P
satisfies the drift condition (
4.2.2
). We let
P^{(\gamma)}
be a discounted transition matrix
P^{(\gamma)}:=\gamma P+(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T}
, where
\gamma\in(0,1]
.

If there exists
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
such that
\sum\limits_{y\in C\cup\Omega}P^{(\gamma)}(x^{*},y)>0
and

\displaystyle\max[\gamma b,(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\mathcal{V}]\leq\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P^{(\gamma)}(x^{*},y)\mathcal{V}(y),

where
\Omega=\left\{x\in\mathcal{X}:\mathcal{V}(x)<\frac{2(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\mathcal{V}}{1-\gamma\varepsilon}\right\}
,
then

\displaystyle\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}[D^{(\gamma)}]\leq\frac{2}{1-\varepsilon\gamma}\left(1+\min\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}[\mathcal{V}(x)]~\|d^{(\gamma)}\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}\right),

where
D^{(\gamma)}
is the group inverse matrix of
I-P^{(\gamma)}
.
The norm of the discounted stationary distribution
\|d^{(\gamma)}\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}
can be bounded as

\displaystyle\|d^{(\gamma)}\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}\leq\frac{2}{1-\varepsilon\gamma}\max[\gamma b,(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\mathcal{V}]\sum\limits_{x\in C\cup\Omega}d^{(\gamma)}(x).

4.3
Policy improvement bound for semi-Markov decision processes

In this section we analyze infinite-horizon semi-Markov decision processes (SMDPs) with
average cost criterion. We derive a policy improvement bound for SMDPs on finite state spaces.

4.3.1
Preliminaries

We consider an SMDP problem model which formulation closely follows the one in
[
109
, Section 11]
. SMDPs generalize MDPs by modeling the sytem evolution in continuous time and allowing the time between state transition to follow an arbitrary probability distribution. Nevertheless, the SMDP should be distinguished from the
natural process
. The
natural process models the state evolution of the system continually throughout time. The SMDP aims to accurately represent the evolution of the system at decision epochs only. Decision epochs occur at random points of time determined by the model description requiring the decision maker to choose actions. We let
\mathcal{X}
be a finite state-space. At decision epoch
t
, the system occupies state
x^{(t)}\in\mathcal{X}
and the decision maker chooses an action
a
from the decision set
\mathcal{A}.
We denote
F(t|x,a)
as the probability that the next decision epoch occurs within
t
time units of the current decision epoch, given that the decision maker chooses action
a
at state
x
at the current decision epoch. We assume that there exist
\epsilon>0
and
\delta>0
such that

\displaystyle F(\delta|x,a)\leq 1-\epsilon,

(4.3.1)

for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

We use
P(y|x,a)
to denote the probability that the SMDP is at state
y
when the next decision epoch occurs, given that the decision maker chooses action
a
at state
x
at the current decision epoch. In other words,
P(y|x,a)
is a transition kernel of the

embedded Markov decision process
, that describes the state transition evolution only, see
[
109
, equation (11.4.5)]
.

We let
t_{k}
be the
k
th decision epoch that happens after time
t_{0}=0
. For convention, we assume that the SMDP starts with the first decision epoch
t_{0}
at time
0
.
At time
t_{k}
, the system occupies state
x^{(t_{k})}
and the decision maker chooses action
a^{(t_{k})}
. As a consequence of this action choice, the system remains in state
x^{(t_{k})}
for
\tau_{k}\sim F(\cdot|x^{(t_{k})},a^{(t_{k})})
units of time, where
F(\cdot|x^{(t_{k})},a^{(t_{k})})
is the cumulative distribution function of
\tau_{k}
. At time
t_{k+1}=t_{k}+\tau_{k}
the system state changes
to
x^{(t_{k+1})}\sim P(\cdot|x^{(t_{k})},a^{(t_{k})})
, and the next decision epoch occurs. We define
m(x,a)
as the expected time until the next decision epoch, given that action
a
is chosen in state
x
at the current decision epoch:

\displaystyle m(x,a):=\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{1}~|~x^{(0)}=x,a^{(0)}=a\right]=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}(1-F(s|x,a))ds.

Below, we also refer to
m:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
as the
expected time function
.
We denote

\displaystyle m_{\pi}(x):=\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}m(x,a)\pi(a|x)

as the expected time until the next decision epoch at state
x\in\mathcal{X}
according to policy
\pi
.

We assume that when action
a
is chosen in state
x
, instantaneous cost
k(x,a)
is incurred. Moreover, infinitesimal cost is incurred at rate
c(y,x,a)
as long as the natural process occupies state
y
, and action
a
was chosen in state
x
at the preceding decision epoch. We define
g(x,a)
as an expected cost that is accumulated between decision epoch when the system is at state
x
and action
a
is chosen, and the following decision epoch:

\displaystyle g(x,a):=k(x,a)+\mathbb{E}\left[\int\limits_{0}^{\tau_{1}}c\left(x^{(t)},x,a\right)dt~|~x^{(0)}=x,a^{(0)}=a\right].

We denote
g_{\pi}(x):=\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}g(x,a)\pi(a|x)
as the expected cost at state
x\in\mathcal{X}
according to policy
\pi
.

We define a randomized stationary Markovian service policy as a map
\pi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{A})
, where
\Delta A
is the probability distribution over action space
\mathcal{A}
.
Under policy
\pi
, the corresponding embedded Markov chain has transition matrix
P_{\pi}
defined as
P_{\pi}(x,y):=\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a|x)P(y|x,a).

We assume that, for every stationary policy, the embedded Markov chain has a
unichain transition probability matrix.

We define a long-run average cost
\eta_{\pi}
of policy
\pi
for an SMDP:

\displaystyle\eta^{\pi}:=\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{(t_{k+1})}\sim P(\cdot|x^{(t_{k})},a^{(t_{k})})\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[g\big(x^{(t_{k})},a^{(t_{k})}\big)~|~x^{(0)}\sim\mu\right]}{\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau_{k}\sim F(\cdot|x^{(t_{k})},a^{(t_{k})})\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\tau_{k}~|~x^{(0)}\sim\mu\right]},

where
\eta^{\pi}
does not depend on the initial state distribution
\mu
since
P_{\pi}
is unichain, see
[
109
, Proposition 11.4.1]
.

We define a Poisson equation of the SMDP with expected cost function
g
, expected time function
m
, and transition kernel
P
for a stationary policy
\pi
:

\displaystyle h(x)=g_{\pi}(x)-\eta m_{\pi}(x)+\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P_{\pi}(y|x)h(y),\text{ for each }x\in\mathcal{X}.

(4.3.2)

By
[
109
, Theorem 11.4.3]
, the long-average cost
\eta_{\pi}
of policy
\pi
and function

\displaystyle h_{\pi}(x):=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[g_{\pi}(x^{(t_{k})})-\eta_{\pi}m_{\pi}(x^{(t_{k})})~|~x^{(0)}=x\right]

(4.3.3)

satisfy Poisson equation (
4.3.2
). Function
h_{\pi}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that satisfies equation (
4.3.2
) is called a solution to the Poisson equation for policy
\pi
.

We define advantage function
A_{\pi}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
of policy
\pi
as

\displaystyle A_{\pi}(x,a):=g(x,a)-\eta_{\pi}m(x,a)+\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(y|x,a)h_{\pi}(y)-h_{\pi}(x),

for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}.

4.3.2
Novel policy improvement bound

In this section we derive a policy improvement bound for SMDPs.
The following lemma establishes performance difference identity for SMDPs. An analogous performance difference identity for MDPs was proposed in
[
64
]
. The proof of Lemma
21
can be found in Appendix
C.3
.

Lemma 21
.

We consider the SMDP model described in Section
4.3.1
. The following policy performance identity holds for any two policies
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
of the SMDP:

\displaystyle\eta_{\pi_{2}}-\eta_{\pi_{1}}=\frac{1}{\overline{m}_{\pi_{2}}}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim d_{\pi_{2}},a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)}\left[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right],

(4.3.4)

where
d_{\pi_{2}}
is the stationary distribution of transition matrix
P_{\pi_{2}}
, and
\overline{m}_{\pi_{2}}:=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim d_{\pi_{2}}}[m_{\pi_{2}}(x)]
is a mean time between decision epochs under policy
\pi_{2}
.

Performance difference identity (
4.3.4
) allows us to establish a policy improvement bound for SMDPs. The proof of Theorem
5
can be found in Appendix
C.3
.

Theorem 5
.

We consider the SMDP model described in Section
4.3.1
. The following bound on the difference of long-run average costs of two policies

\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
holds:

\displaystyle\eta_{\pi_{2}}-\eta_{\pi_{1}}\leq\frac{1}{\overline{m}_{\pi_{2}}}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d_{\pi_{1}}\\
a\sim\pi_{1}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{\pi_{2}(a|x)}{\pi_{1}(a|x)}A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]+2\epsilon_{\pi_{2}}\tau_{1}\left[D_{\pi_{2}}\right]\underset{x\sim d_{\pi_{1}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\text{TV}\Big(\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)~||~\pi_{1}(\cdot|x)\Big)\right],

(4.3.5)

where
D_{\pi_{2}}
is the group inverse of matrix
I-P_{\pi_{2}}
,
\epsilon_{\pi_{2}}:=\frac{1}{\overline{m}_{\pi_{2}}}\max\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Big|\underset{a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)}{\mathbb{E}}[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)]\Big|
.

4.4
Conclusion to Chapter
4

In this chapter we introduce several novel policy improvement bounds for different setting. We get a unified policy improvement bound for discounted and average cost criterions. This new bound refines previous existing policy improvement bounds for MDPs with the discounted objectives and suggests a meaningful bound for the average cost objective. This result clears up the existing doubts about validity of the use of the on-policy deep RL algorithms with a large discount factor and generalizes the use of this class of RL algorithms on MDPs with average cost objectives.

The refined policy improvement bound for the infinite-horizon discounted setting is
optimal
in some sense and depends on the one-norm ergodicity coefficient. We propose several bounds on this ergodicity coefficient to uncover its dependency on the discount factor. Nevertheless, to the best of our knowledge, it is an open problem whether the discounting leads to smaller one-norm ergodicity coefficient, i.e.
\tau_{1}[D_{\pi}]\geq\tau_{1}[D_{\pi}^{(\gamma)}]
for
\gamma<1
. It is known that the discounting does decrease Kemeny’s constant, see
[
24
, Theorem 4.10]
, hence identity (c) from Lemma
17
might be a potential path for proving the equivalent result for the one-norm ergodicity coefficient.

We propose a policy improvement bound for MDPs with countable state spaces. We bound the performance difference between two policies assuming the
\mathcal{V}
-uniform ergodicity of the transition matrix of one of the policies. Additional conditions on the ”closeness” between two policies also makes the bound practical for reinforcement learning. Specifically, APG algorithms can be justified for solving MDP problems on countable state spaces. We obtain several bounds on the
\mathcal{V}
-weighted one-norm ergodicity coefficient that allow to estimate it based on the drift condition satisfied by the corresponding Markov chain. We believe these results are of independent interest for the Markov chains perturbation theory.

Another potential application of the ergodicity coefficient estimation is an adaptive adjustment of the allowed magnitude of the policy changes in each iteration of TRPO, PPO, and similar algorithms. Policy improvement bounds (
4.1.8
), (
4.2.5
), (
4.3.5
) show that the larger the ergodicity coefficient is, the more challenging minimization of the bounds becomes, e.g. in (
2.2.15
).
A large ergodicity coefficient indicates that the corresponding Markov chain is sensitive to the updates and perturbations, and significant changes to its transition probabilities might lead to the performance degradation. While the original TRPO and PPO algorithms suggest fixing a trust region parameter,
\delta
, and a clipping parameter,
\epsilon
, respectively, through the course of learning, we believe that adjustment of these parameters proportionally to the ergodicity coefficient each iteration might improve the robustness and sample complexity. Further research is needed to design and implement TRPO and/or PPO algorithms with the adaptive step sizes depending on the ergodicity coefficient estimates.

Novel results are obtained for SMDPs. We derive the performance difference identity and policy performance bound for SMDP policies. As a result, deep RL algorithms, such as PPO, TRPO and their variations, can be generalized and directly use to solve SMDP problems. Numerical experiments with PPO algorithm on a class of queueing networks with general arrival/service distributions are in our investigation plans.

Appendix A
Chapter 1 of Appendix

A.1
Proofs of the theorems in Section
2.2

Proof of Lemma
3
.

We define vector
h:=Z\left(g-(d^{T}g)e\right)
. Matrix
Z
has a finite
\mathcal{V}-
norm, therefore the inverse matrix of
Z
is unique and equal to
I-P+\Pi
. Then by definition vector
h
satisfies

\displaystyle(I-P+\Pi)h=g-(d^{T}g)e.

(A.1.1)

Multiplying both sides of (
A.1.1
) by
d
we get
d^{T}h=0
(and
\Pi h=0
). Hence, vector
h
is a solution of the Poisson equation (
2.2.5
) such that
d^{T}h=0
. It follows from Lemma
2
that
h=h^{(f)}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4
.

We denote

\displaystyle U_{\theta,\phi}:=(P_{\theta}-P_{\phi})Z_{\phi}

(A.1.2)

and define matrix
H_{\theta,\phi}
as

\displaystyle H_{\theta,\phi}:=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}U^{k}_{\theta,\phi}.

(A.1.3)

The convergence in the
\mathcal{V}
-weighted norm in definition (
A.1.3
) follows from assumption
\|U_{\theta,\phi}\|_{\mathcal{V}}<1
.

The goal of this proof is to show that the Markov chain has a unique stationary distribution
d_{\theta}
such that

\displaystyle d_{\theta}^{T}=d_{\phi}^{T}H_{\theta,\phi}.

(A.1.4)

We let
\nu^{T}:=d_{\phi}^{T}H_{\theta,\phi}
. We use
e=(1,1,...,1,...)^{T}
to denote the unit vector. First, we verify that
\nu^{T}e=\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\nu(x)=1.
We note that
Z_{\phi}e=e.
Then

\displaystyle\nu^{T}e=d_{\phi}^{T}H_{\theta,\phi}e=d_{\phi}^{T}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\left((P_{\theta}-P_{\phi})Z_{\phi}\right)^{k}e=d_{\phi}^{T}Ie=1.

Second, we verify that
\nu^{T}P_{\theta}=\nu^{T}.
We prove it by first assuming
that

\displaystyle P_{\theta}-P_{\phi}+U_{\theta,\phi}P_{\phi}=U_{\theta,\phi}

(A.1.5)

holds. Indeed,

\displaystyle\nu^{T}P_{\theta}

\displaystyle=d_{\phi}^{T}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}U_{\theta,\phi}^{k}P_{\theta}

\displaystyle=d_{\phi}^{T}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}U_{\theta,\phi}^{k}P_{\theta}-d_{\phi}^{T}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}U_{\theta,\phi}^{k}P_{\phi}+d_{\phi}^{T}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}U_{\theta,\phi}^{k}P_{\phi}

\displaystyle=d_{\phi}^{T}+d_{\phi}^{T}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}U_{\theta,\phi}^{k}P_{\theta}-d_{\phi}^{T}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}U_{\theta,\phi}^{k}P_{\phi}+d_{\phi}^{T}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}U_{\theta,\phi}^{k+1}P_{\phi}

\displaystyle=d_{\phi}^{T}+d_{\phi}^{T}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}U_{\theta,\phi}^{k}(P_{\theta}-P_{\phi}+U_{\theta,\phi}P_{\phi})

\displaystyle=d_{\phi}^{T}+d_{\phi}^{T}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}U_{\theta,\phi}^{k+1}

\displaystyle=d_{\phi}^{T}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}U_{\theta,\phi}^{k}

\displaystyle=\nu^{T}.

It remains to prove (
A.1.5
). Indeed,

\displaystyle P_{\theta}-P_{\phi}+U_{\theta,\phi}P_{\phi}

\displaystyle=P_{\theta}-P_{\phi}+(P_{\theta}-P_{\phi})Z_{\phi}P_{\phi}

\displaystyle=(P_{\theta}-P_{\phi})(I+Z_{\phi}P_{\phi})

\displaystyle=(P_{\theta}-P_{\phi})(I-\Pi_{\phi}+Z_{\phi}P_{\phi})

\displaystyle=(P_{\theta}-P_{\phi})(I-Z_{\phi}\Pi_{\phi}+Z_{\phi}P_{\phi})

\displaystyle=(P_{\theta}-P_{\phi})Z_{\phi}

\displaystyle=U_{\theta,\phi},

where the second equality follows from

\displaystyle\Big((P_{\theta}-P_{\phi})Z_{\phi}\Big)P_{\phi}=(P_{\theta}-P_{\phi})\Big(Z_{\phi}P_{\phi}\Big),

which holds by
[
69
, Corollary 1.9]
, the third equality holds due to
(P_{\theta}-P_{\phi})\Pi_{\phi}=0
, the fourth equality holds because
Z_{\phi}\Pi_{\phi}=\Pi_{\phi}
, and the fifth equality follows from
I=Z_{\phi}(I+\Pi_{\phi}-P_{\phi})=Z_{\phi}+Z_{\phi}\Pi_{\phi}-Z_{\phi}P_{\phi}
.

The uniqueness of the stationary distribution follows from the fact that the Markov chain with transition matrix
P_{\theta}
is assumed to be irreducible.

∎

The following Lemma
22
will be used in the proofs of Theorem
1
and Lemma
5
below. We believe the claim of Lemma
22
should be a well-known mathematical fact, but we have not found its proof in any textbook. For completeness, we present it here.

Lemma 22
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}}
be a set of all matrices on the countable space
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
.
The operator norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}}
is equivalent to the operator norm induced from vector norm
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}
and to the operator norm induced from vector norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}
in the following sense:

\displaystyle\|T\|_{\mathcal{V}}=\sup\limits_{\nu:\|\nu\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}=1}\|\nu T\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}=\sup\limits_{h:\|h\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}=1}\|Th\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}\quad\text{ for any }T\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}},

where
\|\nu\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}=\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|\nu(x)|\mathcal{V}(x)
,
\|\nu\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}=\sup\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\frac{|\nu(x)|}{\mathcal{V}(x)}
,
\|T\|_{\mathcal{V}}=\sup\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}(x)}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}|T(x,y)|\mathcal{V}(y)
.

Furthermore, for any vectors
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
on
\mathcal{X}
and matrices
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}}
the following inequalities hold:

\displaystyle\|\nu_{1}^{T}T\nu_{2}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\|\nu_{1}\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}\|T\|_{\mathcal{V}}\|\nu_{2}\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}

(A.1.6)

and

\displaystyle\|T_{1}T_{2}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\|T_{1}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\|T_{2}\|_{\mathcal{V}}.

(A.1.7)

Proof.

First, we show that
\sup\limits_{h:\|h\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}=1}\|Th\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}=\|T\|_{\mathcal{V}}
. On the one hand,

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{\nu:\|\nu\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}=1}\|\nu T\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{\nu:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\|\nu\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}}\|\nu T\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\geq\sup\limits_{\nu\in\{e_{x}\}}\frac{1}{\|\nu\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}}\|\nu T\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}(x)}\sum\limits_{y\in X}\mathcal{V}(y)|T(x,y)|=\|T\|_{\mathcal{V}},

where in the second step we choose a set
\{e_{x}\}
of unit vectors
e_{x}=(0,\dotsc,0,1,0,\dotsc)
, where the
x
coordinate is
1
and the other coordinates are
0
s.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\|T\|_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{x\in X}\sum\limits_{y\in X}\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}(x)}|T(x,y)|\mathcal{V}(y)\sup\limits_{\nu:\|\nu\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}=1}\sum\limits_{x\in X}|\nu(x)|\mathcal{V}(x)

\displaystyle\geq\sup\limits_{\nu:\|\nu\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}=1}\sum\limits_{y\in X}\sum\limits_{x\in X}\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}(x)}~|T(x,y)|~\mathcal{V}(y)~|\nu(x)|~\mathcal{V}(x)

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{\nu:\|\nu\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}=1}\sum\limits_{y\in X}\sum\limits_{x\in X}|T(x,y)\nu(x)|\mathcal{V}(y)

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{\nu:\|\nu\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}=1}\|\nu T\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}.

Similarly, we can show the equivalency of
\sup\limits_{h:\|h\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}=1}\|Th\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}
and
\|T\|_{\mathcal{V}}
norms.

Inequalities (
A.1.6
) and (
A.1.7
) follow from the properties of a linear operator norm.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1
.

We denote
U_{\theta,\phi}:=(P_{\theta}-P_{\phi})Z_{\phi}
. Under assumption
\|U_{\theta,\phi}\|_{\mathcal{V}}=D_{\theta,\phi}<1
operator
H_{\theta,\phi}:=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}U^{k}_{\theta,\phi}
is well-defined and

\displaystyle\|H_{\theta,\phi}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\frac{1}{1-D_{\theta,\phi}}.

(A.1.8)

We represent the stationary distribution of the Markov chain with transition matrix
P_{\theta}
as
d_{\theta}^{T}=d_{\phi}^{T}H_{\theta,\phi}
, see (
A.1.4
). We get

\displaystyle\|d_{\theta}\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}=d_{\theta}^{T}\mathcal{V}=d_{\phi}H_{\theta,\phi}\mathcal{V}\leq\|H_{\theta,\phi}\|_{\mathcal{V}}(d_{\phi}^{T}\mathcal{V})<\infty,

(A.1.9)

since
H_{\theta,\phi}\mathcal{V}\leq\|H_{\theta,\phi}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{V}
by definition of the
\mathcal{V}
-norm.

The long-run average costs difference is equal to

\displaystyle d_{\theta}^{T}g-d_{\phi}^{T}g

\displaystyle=d_{\theta}^{T}g+d_{\theta}^{T}\left((P_{\theta}-I)h_{\phi}\right)-d_{\phi}^{T}g

\displaystyle=d_{\theta}^{T}(g+P_{\theta}h_{\phi}-h_{\phi})-d_{\phi}^{T}g

\displaystyle=d_{\phi}^{T}(g-\eta_{\phi}e+P_{\theta}h_{\phi}-h_{\phi})+(d_{\theta}^{T}-d_{\phi}^{T})(g+P_{\theta}h_{\phi}-h_{\phi})

\displaystyle=d_{\phi}^{T}(g-\eta_{\phi}e+P_{\theta}h_{\phi}-h_{\phi})+(d_{\theta}^{T}-d_{\phi}^{T})(g-\eta_{\phi}e+P_{\theta}h_{\phi}-h_{\phi}).

Now we are ready to bound the last term:

\displaystyle\left|(d_{\theta}^{T}-d_{\phi}^{T})(g-\eta_{\phi}e+P_{\theta}h_{\phi}-h_{\phi})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\|d_{\theta}-d_{\phi}\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}~\|g-(d_{\phi}^{T}g)e+P_{\theta}h_{\phi}-h_{\phi}\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\|d_{\theta}-d_{\phi}\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}~\|(P_{\theta}-P_{\phi})h_{\phi}\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\|d_{\theta}-d_{\phi}\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}~\|(P_{\theta}-P_{\phi})Z_{\phi}(g-(d_{\phi}^{T}g)e)\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\leq\|d_{\theta}-d_{\phi}\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}~\|(P_{\theta}-P_{\phi})Z_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{V}}~\|g-\eta_{\phi}e\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=D_{\theta,\phi}\|d_{\theta}-d_{\phi}\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}~\|g-\eta_{\phi}e\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=D_{\theta,\phi}\|d_{\theta}^{T}U_{\theta,\phi}\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}~\|g-\eta_{\phi}e\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}},

\displaystyle\leq D_{\theta,\phi}\|d_{\theta}\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}\|U_{\theta,\phi}\|_{\mathcal{V}}~\|g-\eta_{\phi}e\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}},

\displaystyle\leq D_{\theta,\phi}^{2}\|d_{\theta}\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}\|g-\eta_{\phi}e\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}},

\displaystyle\leq D^{2}_{\theta,\phi}\|H_{\theta,\phi}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\|g-\eta_{\phi}e\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}(d_{\phi}^{T}\mathcal{V}),

\displaystyle\leq\frac{D^{2}_{\theta,\phi}}{1-D_{\theta,\phi}}\|g-\eta_{\phi}e\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}(d_{\phi}^{T}\mathcal{V}),

where the first, second and third inequalities follow from Lemma
22
, the second equality follows from Lemma
3
, the last equality holds due to

d_{\theta}^{T}-d_{\phi}^{T}=d_{\theta}^{T}U_{\theta,\phi}

from (
A.1.4
), the fourth inequality follows from (
2.2.10
), the fifth inequality follows from (
A.1.9
), and the last inequality holds due to (
A.1.8
).

∎

Proof of Lemma
5
.

\displaystyle\|(P_{\theta}-P_{\phi})Z_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\leq\|P_{\theta}-P_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\|Z_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\|Z_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\sup\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}(x)}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}|P_{\theta}-P_{\phi}|_{x,y}\mathcal{V}(y)

\displaystyle=\|Z_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\sup\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}(x)}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}|\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}P(y|x,a)\pi_{\theta}(a|x)-\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}P(y|x,a)\pi_{\phi}(a|x)|\mathcal{V}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\|Z_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\sup\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}(x)}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}P(y|x,a)|\pi_{\theta}(a|x)-\pi_{\phi}(a|x)|\mathcal{V}(y)

\displaystyle=\|Z_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\sup\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}|\pi_{\theta}(a|x)-\pi_{\phi}(a|x)|\frac{\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(y|x,a)\mathcal{V}(y)}{\mathcal{V}(x)}

\displaystyle=\|Z_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\sup\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\Big|\frac{\pi_{\theta}(a|x)}{\pi_{\phi}(a|x)}-1\Big|G(x,a)

∎

A.2
Proofs of the theorems in Section
2.3

We consider the Poisson equation for a Markov chain with the transition kernel
P
, stationary distribution
d
, and cost function
g:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle g(x)-d^{T}g+\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(y|x)h(y)-h(x)=0,\text{ for each }x\in\mathcal{X},

which admits a solution

\displaystyle h^{(x^{*})}(x):=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\left(g(x^{(k)})-d^{T}g\right)|x^{(0)}=x\right]\text{ for each }x\in\mathcal{X},

where
\sigma(x^{*})=\min\left\{k>0~|~x^{(k)}=x^{*}\right\}
is the first time when state
x^{*}
is visited.

Since regenerative cycles can be long in large-size systems, we propose to change the original dynamics and increase the probability of transition to the regenerative state
x^{*}
from each state
x\in\mathcal{X}.

We let
P(y|x)
be an original transition probability from state
x
to state
y
, for each
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
. We consider a new Markov reward process with cost function
g
and a modified transition kernel
\tilde{P}^{(\gamma)}
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\tilde{P}^{(\gamma)}(y|x):=\gamma P(y|x)\quad\text{ for }y\neq x^{*},\\
\tilde{P}^{(\gamma)}(x^{*}|x):=\gamma P(x^{*}|x)+(1-\gamma),\end{cases}

(A.2.1)

for each
x\in\mathcal{X}.

We modified the transition kernel so that the probability of transition to the regenerative state
x^{*}
is at least
1-\gamma
from any state.

The Poisson equation for the modified problem is equal to:

\displaystyle g(x)-\tilde{d}^{T}g+\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\tilde{P}^{(\gamma)}(y|x)\tilde{h}(y)-\tilde{h}(x)=0,\text{ for each }x\in\mathcal{X},

(A.2.2)

where
\tilde{d}
is the stationary distribution of the Markov chain
\tilde{P}^{(\gamma)}
.

Equation (
A.2.2
) admits a solution

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{(x^{*})}(x):=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\tilde{\sigma}(x^{*})-1}\left(g(x^{(k)})-\tilde{d}^{T}g\right)~|~x^{(0)}=x\right]\text{ for each }x\in\mathcal{X},

(A.2.3)

where
x^{(k)}
is the state of the Markov chain with transition matrix
\tilde{P}^{(\gamma)}
after
k
timesteps, and
\tilde{\sigma}(x^{*})=\min\left\{k>0~|~x^{(k)}=x^{*}\right\}
.
According to the new dynamics the regeneration occurs more frequently and we can estimate solution (
A.2.3
) by using fewer replications of the regenerative simulation.

Lemma 23
.

Consider the Poisson equation for the Markov chain with the transition kernel
\tilde{P}^{(\gamma)}
defined by (
A.2.1
), stationary distribution
\tilde{d}
, and cost function
g:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle g(x)-\tilde{d}^{T}g+\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\tilde{P}^{(\gamma)}(y|x)\tilde{h}(y)-\tilde{h}(x)=0,\text{ for each }x\in\mathcal{X}.

(A.2.4)

Equation (
A.2.4
) admits solutions:

\displaystyle J^{(\gamma)}(x):=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{k}\left(g(x^{(k)})-d^{T}g\right)~|~x^{(0)}=x\right]\text{ for each }x\in\mathcal{X},

and

\displaystyle V^{(\gamma)}(x):=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\gamma^{k}\left(g(x^{(k)})-r(x^{*})\right)~|~x^{(0)}=x\right]\text{ for each }x\in\mathcal{X},

where
x^{(k)}
is the state of the Markov chain with transition matrix
P
after
k
timesteps.

Proof.

We substitute the definition of
\tilde{P}^{(\gamma)}
(
A.2.1
) and rewrite equation (
A.2.4
) as

\displaystyle g(x)-(\tilde{d}^{T}g-(1-\gamma)\tilde{h}(x^{*}))+\gamma\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(y|x)\tilde{h}(y)-\tilde{h}(x)=0,\text{ for each }x\in\mathcal{X}.

(A.2.5)

Equation (
A.2.5
) admits infinitely many solutions, but we specify a unique solution fixing
\tilde{h}(x^{*}).

Next, we consider two options.

First, we let
\tilde{h}(x^{*})=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}(d^{T}g-\tilde{d}^{T}g)
. Then the Poisson equation (
A.2.5
) becomes

\displaystyle g(x)-d^{T}g+\gamma\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(y|x)\tilde{h}(y)-\tilde{h}(x)=0,\text{ for each }x\in\mathcal{X},

and admits solution

\displaystyle J^{(\gamma)}(x):=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{k}\left(g(x^{(k)})-d^{T}g\right)~|~x^{(0)}=x\right]\text{ for each }x\in\mathcal{X}.

Second, we let
\tilde{h}(x^{*})=0
in equation (
A.2.5
). We note that
\tilde{d}^{T}g=r(x^{*})
, where
r(x^{*})=(1-\gamma)\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{k}g\left(x^{(k)}\right)~|~x^{(0)}=x^{*}\right]
is a present discounted value at
x^{*}.

We get the Poisson equation (
2.3.16
)

\displaystyle g(x)-r(x^{*})+\gamma\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(y|x)\tilde{h}(y)-\tilde{h}(x)=0,\text{ for each }x\in\mathcal{X},

which admits solution (
2.3.11
)

\displaystyle V^{(\gamma)}(x):=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\gamma^{k}\left(g(x^{(k)})-r(x^{*})\right)|x^{(0)}=x\right]\text{ for each }x\in\mathcal{X}.

Indeed,

\displaystyle V^{(\gamma)}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{k}\left(g(x^{(k)})-r(x^{*})\right)|x^{(0)}=x\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\gamma^{k}\left(g(x^{(k)})-r(x^{*})\right)|x^{(0)}=x\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\gamma^{\sigma(x^{*})}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{k}\left(g(x^{(k)})-r(x^{*})\right)|x^{(0)}=x^{*}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\gamma^{k}\left(g(x^{(k)})-r(x^{*})\right)|x^{(0)}=x\right].

∎

Proof of Lemma
7
.

By Lemma
23
function
V^{(\gamma)}
is a solution of Poisson equation (
A.2.2
). We consider the discounted value function
J^{(\gamma)}=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{k}\left(g\left(x^{(k)}\right)-d^{T}g\right)~|~x^{(0)}=x\right]
that is another solution.

Then, for an arbitrary
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle\left|V^{(\gamma)}(x)-h^{(x^{*})}(x)\right|\leq\left|J^{(\gamma)}(x)-h^{(f)}(x)\right|+\left|V^{(\gamma)}(x)-h^{(x^{*})}(x)-\left(J^{(\gamma)}(x)-h^{(f)}(x)\right)\right|,

(A.2.6)

where
h^{(f)}
is the fundamental solution of the Poisson equation (
2.2.5
).

First, we bound
\left|J^{(\gamma)}(x)-h^{(f)}(x)\right|
:

\displaystyle|J^{(\gamma)}(x)-h^{(f)}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}|\gamma^{t}-1|\Big|\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P^{t}(y|x)(g(y)-d^{T}g)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq R\mathcal{V}(x)\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}(1-\gamma^{t})r^{t}

\displaystyle=R\mathcal{V}(x)r\frac{1-\gamma}{(1-r)(1-\gamma r)},

where the second inequality follows from (
2.2.4
).

Since
V^{(\gamma)}
and
J^{(\gamma)}
are both solutions of the Poisson equation (
A.2.2
), therefore,

\displaystyle J^{(\gamma)}(x)-V^{(\gamma)}(x)=J^{(\gamma)}(x^{*})-V^{(\gamma)}(x^{*})=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}(d^{T}g-r(x^{*}))\text{ for each }x\in\mathcal{X}.

Similarly,
h^{(f)}(x)-h^{(x^{*})}(x)=h^{(f)}(x^{*})
for each
x\in\mathcal{X}.

Second, we bound the last term in inequality (
A.2.6
):

\displaystyle\Big|V^{(\gamma)}(x)-h^{(x^{*})}(x)

\displaystyle-\left(J^{(\gamma)}(x)-h^{(f)}(x)\right)\Big|

\displaystyle=\left|h^{(f)}(x^{*})-\frac{1}{1-\gamma}(r(x^{*})-d^{T}g)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P^{t}(y|x^{*})(g(y)-d^{T}g)-\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\gamma^{t}P^{t}(y|x^{*})(g(y)-d^{T}g)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}(1-\gamma^{t})P^{t}(y|x^{*})(g(y)-d^{T}g)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}|1-\gamma^{t}|\left|\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P^{t}(y|x^{*})(g(y)-d^{T}g)\right|

\displaystyle\leq R\mathcal{V}(x^{*})r\frac{1-\gamma}{(1-r)(1-\gamma r)},

where the last inequality holds due to (
2.2.4
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
8
.

We denote
\overline{g}(x^{(k)}):=\left(g(x^{(k)})-r(x^{*})\right)
.
Following
[
94
, Section 17.4.3]
, we can show that

\displaystyle Var[\hat{V}^{(\gamma)}(x)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\gamma^{k}\overline{g}(x^{(k)})\right)^{2}~\Big|~x^{(0)}=x\right]-\left(V^{(\gamma)}(x)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\gamma^{2k}\overline{g}^{2}(x^{(k)})+2\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\sum\limits_{j=k}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\gamma^{k+j}\overline{g}(x^{(k)})\overline{g}(x^{(j)})~\Big|~x^{(0)}=x\right]-\left(V^{(\gamma)}(x)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\mathbb{E}\left[2\sum\limits_{j=k}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\gamma^{k+j}\overline{g}(x^{(k)})\overline{g}(x^{(j)})-\gamma^{2k}\overline{g}^{2}(x^{(k)})~|~\mathcal{F}_{k}\right]~\Big|~x^{(0)}=x\right]-\left(V^{(\gamma)}(x)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}2\gamma^{2k}\overline{g}(x^{(k)})\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{j=k}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\gamma^{j-k}\overline{g}(x^{(j)})~|~\mathcal{F}_{k}\right]-\gamma^{2k}\overline{g}^{2}(x^{(k)})~\Big|~x^{(0)}=x\right]-\left(V^{(\gamma)}(x)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}2\gamma^{2k}\overline{g}(x^{(k)})V^{(\gamma)}(x^{(k)})-\gamma^{2k}\overline{g}^{2}(x^{(k)})~\Big|~x^{(0)}=x\right]-\left(V^{(\gamma)}(x)\right)^{2},

where
\mathcal{F}_{k}
is a
\sigma-
algebra generated by
x^{(0)},x^{(1)},...,x^{(k)}
.

Hereafter, we denote
\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(y|x)V^{(\gamma)}(y)
and
\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(y|x)\left(V^{(\gamma)}(y)\right)^{2}
as
PV^{(\gamma)}(x)
and
P\left(V^{(\gamma)}\right)^{2}(x)
, respectively, to improve readability. We use the Poisson equation (
2.3.16
) and replace
\overline{g}(x^{(k)})
by
V^{(\gamma)}(x^{(k)})-\gamma PV^{(\gamma)}(x^{(k)})
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}2\gamma^{2k}\overline{g}(x^{(k)})V^{(\gamma)}(x^{(k)})-\gamma^{2k}\overline{g}^{2}(x^{(k)})~\Big|~x^{(0)}=x\right]-\left(V^{(\gamma)}(x)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\gamma^{2k}\left(2\left(V^{(\gamma)}(x^{(k)})-\gamma PV^{(\gamma)}(x^{(k)})\right)V^{(\gamma)}(x_{k})-\left(V^{(\gamma)}(x^{(k)})-\gamma PV^{(\gamma)}(x^{(k)})\right)^{2}\right)~\Big|~x^{(0)}=x\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\left(V^{(\gamma)}(x)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\gamma^{2k}\left(\left(V^{(\gamma)}(x^{(k)})\right)^{2}-\left(\gamma PV^{(\gamma)}(x^{(k)})\right)^{2}\right)~\Big|~x^{(0)}=x\right]-\left(V^{(\gamma)}(x)\right)^{2}.

Next, we subtract the expectation of a martingale

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[-\left(V^{(\gamma)}(x)\right)^{2}+\gamma^{2\sigma(x^{*})}\left(V^{(\gamma)}(x^{(\sigma(x^{*}))})\right)^{2}+\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\gamma^{2k}\left(\left(V^{(\gamma)}(x^{(k)})\right)^{2}-\gamma^{2}P\left(V^{(\gamma)}\right)^{2}(x^{(k)})\right)~\Big|~x^{(0)}=x\right]

that is equal to zero by
[
54
, Proposition 1]
. Since
V^{(\gamma)}(x^{(\sigma(x^{*}))})=0
, we get

\displaystyle Var[\hat{V}^{(\gamma)}(x)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\gamma^{2k}\left(\gamma^{2}P\left(V^{(\gamma)}\right)^{2}(x^{(k)})-\left(\gamma PV^{(\gamma)}(x^{(k)})\right)^{2}\right)~\Big|~x^{(0)}=x\right]

\displaystyle=\gamma^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\gamma^{2k}\left(Var\left[V^{(\gamma)}(x^{(k+1)})~|~x^{(k)}\right]\right)~\Big|~x^{(0)}=x\right],

where
Var\left[V^{(\gamma)}(x^{(k+1)})~|~x^{(k)}\right]=\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(y|x^{(k)})\left(V^{(\gamma)}(y)\right)^{2}-\left(\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(y|x^{(k)})V^{(\gamma)}(y)\right)^{2}.

Next, we want to show that there exists constant
B_{1}>0
such that for any
\gamma\in[0,1]

\displaystyle\left(V^{(\gamma)}(x)\right)^{2}\leq B_{1}\mathcal{V}(x)\quad\text{for each }x\in\mathcal{X}.

First, we recall that function
V^{(\gamma)}
is a solution of Poisson equation (
A.2.2
) for the system with modified dynamics (
A.2.1
) and cost function
g
. Given that transition matrix
P
satisfies the drift condition (
2.2.1
), for the modified dynamics we have the following drift inequality

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\tilde{P}^{(\gamma)}(y|x)\mathcal{V}(y)\leq\varepsilon\mathcal{V}(x)+(\mathcal{V}(x^{*})+b)\mathbb{I}_{C\cup\{x:b\mathcal{V}(x)\leq\mathcal{V}(x^{*})\}}(x)\quad\text{for each }x\in\mathcal{X},

for any
\gamma\in[0,1].

Indeed,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\tilde{P}^{(\gamma)}(y|x)\mathcal{V}(y)

\displaystyle=\gamma\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(y|x)\mathcal{V}(y)+(1-\gamma)\mathcal{V}(x^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\varepsilon\mathcal{V}(x)+(1-\gamma)\mathcal{V}(x^{*})+b\mathbb{I}_{C}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\mathcal{V}(x)+(\mathcal{V}(x^{*})+b)\mathbb{I}_{C\cup\{x\in\mathcal{X}:\varepsilon\mathcal{V}(x)\leq\mathcal{V}(x^{*})\}}(x),

where the first inequality follows from the drift condition (
2.2.1
), and the second inequality follows from the fact that
\gamma\varepsilon\mathcal{V}(x)+(1-\gamma)\mathcal{V}(x^{*})\leq\varepsilon\mathcal{V}(x)
if
\varepsilon\mathcal{V}(x)\geq\mathcal{V}(x^{*})
, and
\gamma\varepsilon\mathcal{V}(x)+(1-\gamma)\mathcal{V}(x^{*})\leq\mathcal{V}(x^{*})
otherwise.

Second, we use Jensen’s inequality and get that function
\sqrt{\mathcal{V}}
is also a Lyapunov function for the modified system:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\tilde{P}^{(\gamma)}(y|x)\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(y)}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon\mathcal{V}(x)}+\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(x^{*})+b}~\mathbb{I}_{C\cup\{x\in\mathcal{X}:\varepsilon\mathcal{V}(x)\leq\mathcal{V}(x^{*})\}}(x)\quad\text{for each }x\in\mathcal{X}.

This drift inequality and the assumption that
|g(x)|\leq\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(x)}
for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
allow us to apply
[
94
, Theorem 17.7.1]
, see also
[
94
, equation (17.39)]
, and conclude that, for some
c_{0}>0
independent of
\gamma
, Poisson equation (
A.2.2
) admits the fundamental solution
J^{(\gamma)}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle|J^{(\gamma)}(x)|\leq c_{0}(\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(x)}+1),\text{ for each }x\in\mathcal{X}.

Function
V^{(\gamma)}
is another solution of Poisson equation (
A.2.2
), such that
J^{(\gamma)}(x)=V^{(\gamma)}(x)+J^{(\gamma)}(x^{*})
, for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
, because
V^{(\gamma)}(x^{*})=0
. Since
\mathcal{V}\geq 1
, there exists constant
B_{1}>0
such that

\displaystyle|V^{(\gamma)}(x)|\leq|J^{(\gamma)}(x)|+|J^{(\gamma)}(x^{*})|\leq c_{0}(\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(x)}+1)+c_{0}(\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(x^{*})}+1)\leq\sqrt{B_{1}\mathcal{V}(x)},

for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
We have proved that, for some constant
B_{1}>0
,

\displaystyle\left(V^{(\gamma)}(x)\right)^{2}\leq B_{1}\mathcal{V}(x)\quad\text{for each }x\in\mathcal{X},

for any
\gamma\in[0,1].

We let
G^{(\gamma)}(x):=Var\left[V^{(\gamma)}(x^{(1)})~|~x^{(0)}=x\right].
Then there exists a positive constant
B
such that

\displaystyle G^{(\gamma)}(x)\leq\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(y|x)\left(V^{(\gamma)}(y)\right)^{2}\leq B_{1}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(y|x)\mathcal{V}(y)\leq B\mathcal{V}(x),

(A.2.7)

where the last inequality follows from the drift condition (
2.2.1
).
By
[
94
, Theorem 15.0.1]
we have that there exist constants
R>0
and
r\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle\left|\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P^{k}(y|x)G^{(\gamma)}(y)-\beta^{(\gamma)}\right|\leq R\mathcal{V}(x)r^{k},

(A.2.8)

where
\beta^{(\gamma)}:=\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}d(x)G^{(\gamma)}(x)
is a discounted asymptotic variance.

Then

\displaystyle Var[\hat{V}^{(\gamma)}(x)]

\displaystyle=\gamma^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\sigma(x^{*})-1}\gamma^{2k}\left(Var\left[V^{(\gamma)}(x^{(k+1)})~|~x^{(k)}\right]\right)~\Big|~x^{(0)}=x\right]

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{2}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{2k}\mathbb{E}[G^{(\gamma)}(x_{k})~|~x^{(0)}=x]

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{2}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{2k}\left(R\mathcal{V}(x)r^{k}+\beta^{(\gamma)}\right)

\displaystyle=\gamma^{2}\left(R\mathcal{V}(x)\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{2}r}+\beta^{(\gamma)}\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{2}\left(R\mathcal{V}(x)\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{2}r}+(d^{T}\mathcal{V})B\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{2}}\right),

where the second inequality follows from (
A.2.8
) and the last inequality follows from (
A.2.7
).

∎

A.3
Maximal stability of the proportionally randomized policy

We assert that a discrete-time MDP obtained by
uniformization of the multiclass queueing network semi-Markov decision process model is stable
under the proportionally randomized (PR) policy if the load conditions
(
2.1.9
) are satisfied. We illustrate the proof for the criss-cross
queueing network. We let
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{3}
be a state for the discrete-time
MDP. The proportionally randomized policy
\pi
is given by

\displaystyle\pi(x)=\begin{cases}\Big(\frac{x_{1}}{x_{1}+x_{3}},1,\frac{x_{3}}{x_{1}+x_{3}}\Big)&\text{ if }x_{2}\geq 1\text{ and }x_{1}+x_{3}\geq 1,\\
\Big(\frac{x_{1}}{x_{1}+x_{3}},0,\frac{x_{3}}{x_{1}+x_{3}}\Big)&\text{ if }x_{2}=0\text{ and }x_{1}+x_{3}\geq 1,\\
\big(0,1,0\big)&\text{ if }x_{2}\geq 1\text{ and }x_{1}+x_{3}=0,\\
\big(0,0,0\big)&\text{ if }x_{2}=0\text{ and }x_{1}+x_{3}=0.\end{cases}

Recall the transition probabilities
\tilde{P}
defined by (
2.1.5
). The
discrete-time MDP operating under policy
\pi
is a DTMC. Now we can specify its
transition matrix. For
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{3}
with
x_{1}\geq 1
,
x_{3}\geq 1
, and
x_{2}\geq 1
,

\displaystyle P(y|x)

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}(x)\tilde{P}\big(y|x,(1,2)\big)+\pi_{3}(x)\tilde{P}\big(y|x,(3,2)\big)\quad\text{ for each }y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{3}.

For
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{3}
on the boundary with
x_{1}\geq 1
,
x_{3}\geq 1
, and
x_{2}=0
,

\displaystyle P(y|x)

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}(x)\tilde{P}\big(y|x,(1,0)\big)+\pi_{3}(x)\tilde{P}\big(y|x,(3,0)\big)\quad\text{ for each }y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{3}.

For
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{3}
on the boundary with
x_{1}=0
,
x_{3}\geq 1
, and
x_{2}\geq 1
,

\displaystyle P(y|x)

\displaystyle=\tilde{P}\big(y|x,(3,2)\big)\quad\text{ for each }y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{3}.

Similarly, we write the transition probabilities for other boundary cases. One can verify that

\displaystyle P\big((x_{1}+1,x_{2},x_{3})|x\big)=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{B},\quad P\big((x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}+1)|x\big)=\frac{\lambda_{3}}{B},

(A.3.1)

\displaystyle P\big((x_{1}-1,x_{2}+1,x_{3})|x\big)=\frac{\mu_{1}}{B}\frac{x_{1}}{x_{1}+x_{3}}\quad\text{ if }x_{2}\geq 1,

(A.3.2)

\displaystyle P\big((x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}-1)|x\big)=\frac{\mu_{3}}{B}\frac{x_{3}}{x_{1}+x_{3}}\quad\text{ if }x_{3}\geq 1,

(A.3.3)

\displaystyle P\big((x_{1},x_{2}-1,x_{3})|x\big)=\frac{\mu_{2}}{B}\quad\text{ if }x_{2}\geq 1,

(A.3.4)

\displaystyle P(x|x)=1-\sum_{y\neq x}P(y|x),

(A.3.5)

where
B=\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{3}+\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\mu_{3}
. This transition
matrix
P
is irreducible. Now we consider the continuous-time
criss-cross network operating under the head-of-line
proportional-processor-sharing (HLPPS) policy defined in

[
19
]
. Under the HLPPS policy, the jobcount process

\{Z(t),t\geq 0\}
is a CTMC. Under the load condition (
2.1.1
),

[
19
]
proves that the CTMC is positive recurrent. One can
verify that the transition probabilities in
(
A.3.1
)-(
A.3.5
) are identical to the ones for a
uniformized DTMC of this CTMC. Therefore, the DTMC corresponding to the
transition probabilities (
A.3.1
)-(
A.3.5
) is positive
recurrent, and proves the stability of the discrete-time MDP operating under the
proportionally randomized policy.

A.4
Additional experimental results

In Remark
5
we discussed two possible biased
estimators of the solution to the Poisson equation. In this section we
compare the performance of the PPO algorithm with these two
estimators. We consider two versions of line 7 in Algorithm
2.3.3
: Version 1 uses the regenerative discounted value function
(VF) estimator (
2.3.20
), and Version 2 uses the discounted value
function estimator (
2.3.23
). We apply two versions of the PPO
algorithm for the criss-cross network operating under the balanced medium (B.M.) load
regime. The queueing network parameter setting is identical to
the one detailed in Section
2.4.1
, except that the quadratic
cost function
g(x)=x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}
replaces the linear
cost function that is used to minimize the long-run average cost, where

x_{i}
is a number of jobs in buffer
i
,
i=1,2,3.

We use Xavier initialization to initialize
the policy NN parameters
\theta_{0}
. We take the empty system state
x^{*}=(0,0,0)
as a regeneration state. Each episode in each iteration starts at the regenerative state and runs for
6,000
timesteps. We compute the one-replication estimates of a value function (either regenerative discounted VF or discounted VF) for the first
N=5,000
steps at each episode. In this experiment we simulated
Q=20
episodes in parallel. The values of the remaining hyperparameters (not mentioned yet) are the same as in Table
A.6
.

In Figure
A.4
we compare the learning curves of PPO algorithm
2.3.3
empirically to demonstrate the benefits of using the regenerative discounted VF estimator over the discounted VF estimator when the system regeneration occurs frequently.

2.3.3
for the criss-cross network with the B.M. load and quadratic cost function.]

in which
