A.5
Neural network structure

In the experiments we parameterized the RL policy with a neural
network. Here, we use
\theta
to denote the vector of weights and
biases of the neural network. For a fixed parameter
\theta
, the
neural network outputs deterministically distribution

\pi_{\theta}(\cdot|x)
over the action space for each state

x\in\mathcal{X}
. Therefore, the resulting policy
\pi_{\theta}
is a randomized policy as explained in Section
2.1
.

To represent the policy we use a fully connected feed-forward neural network with one input layer, three hidden layers with tanh activation functions, and one output layer. The input layer has
J
units, one for each job class, the first hidden layer has
10\times J
units, the third hidden layer has
10\times L
, where
L
is number of stations in the queueing system. The number of units in the second hidden layer is a geometric mean of units in the first and third hidden layers (i.e.
10\times\sqrt{LJ}
).

We use
z^{(k)}_{j}
to denote the variable in the
j
th unit of hidden
layer
k
,
k=1,2,3
. Thus, our feed-forward neural network has the following representations:

\displaystyle z^{(1)}_{j}=h\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{J}A^{(1)}_{ji}x_{i}+b^{(1)}_{j}\Big),\quad j=1,\ldots,10J,

\displaystyle z^{(2)}_{j}=h\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{10J}A^{(2)}_{ji}z^{(1)}_{i}+b^{(2)}_{j}\Big),\quad j=1,\ldots,10\sqrt{LJ},

\displaystyle z^{(3)}_{j}=h\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{10\sqrt{LJ}}A^{(3)}_{ji}z^{(2)}_{i}+b^{(3)}_{j}\Big),\quad j=1,\ldots,10\sqrt{L},

where
h:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is the activation function given by
h(y)=\tanh(y)
for each
y\in\mathbb{R}
.

We denote
p=(p_{j})
as the output vector, which is given by

\displaystyle p_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{10\sqrt{L}}A^{(4)}_{ji}z^{(3)}_{i}+b^{(4)}_{j},\quad j\in\mathcal{J},

and normalize output vector
p
via a
softmax function
into
L
probability
distributions as:

\displaystyle\pi_{\theta}(j|x)=\frac{\exp(p_{j})}{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{L}(\ell)}\exp(p_{i})}\quad\text{ for each }j\in\mathcal{B}(\ell),\,\ell\in\mathcal{L},

(A.5.1)

where the sets
\mathcal{J}
,
\mathcal{L}
, and
\mathcal{B}(\ell)
are defined in Section
2.1
.

The neural network parameter
\theta
is the vector of weights
A
’s and biases
b
’s

\displaystyle\theta=\Big(A^{(1)},\,b^{(1)},A^{(2)},\,b^{(2)},A^{(3)},\,b^{(3)},A^{(4)},\,b^{(4)}\Big),

which has dimension

\displaystyle K=\big(J\times 10J+10J\big)+\big(10J\times 10\sqrt{LJ}+10\sqrt{LJ}\big)+\big(10\sqrt{LJ}\times 10L+10L\big)+\big(10L\times J+J\big).

For example, when
J=21
and
L=7
, this dimension is approximately equal to

30203
.

To represent the value function we use a neural network whose
architecture is almost identical to the policy neural network except
that the third hidden layer has
10
units. The
number of units in the second hidden layer is
10\times\sqrt{J}
. The
output layer contains one unit with a linear activation function, which means
that

\displaystyle V(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{10}A^{(4)}_{i}z^{(3)}_{i}+b^{(4)}.

For the N-model processing network in Section
2.4.3
the structure of policy and value NNs is the same as for the MQNs described above, except for the meaning of set
\mathcal{B}(\ell)
in (
A.5.1
). We consider station
\ell\in\mathcal{L}
of a processing network. The set
\mathcal{B}(\ell)
includes a job class if and only if there is an activity such that station
\ell
can process jobs from this class.

A.6
Implementation details of numerical experiments in Section
2.4

We use Tensorflow v1.13.1
[
1
]
to build a training routine of the neural networks and Ray package v0.6.6
[
97
]

to maintain parallel simulation of the actors. We run all experiments on a 2.7 GHz 96-core processor with 1510 GB of RAM.

We optimize the value and policy functions to minimize the corresponding loss functions (
2.3.4
), (
2.3.7
) by the Adaptive Moment Estimation (Adam) method
[
70
]
.
The Adam method is an algorithm for mini-batch gradient-based optimization.
We assume that
N
datapoints have been generated
D^{(1:N)}=\Big\{(x^{(j)},a^{(j)},\hat{A}_{j})\Big\}_{j=1}^{N}
to update the policy NN or
D^{(1:N)}=\left\{(x^{(j)},\hat{V}_{j})\right\}_{j=1}^{N}
to update the value NN.
The Adam algorithm runs for
E
epochs. The number of epochs is the number of complete passes through the entire dataset. In the beginning of a new epoch
e
the entire dataset
is randomly reshuffled and divided into batches with size
m
. Then each batch (indexed by
n
) is passed to the learning algorithm and the parameters
of the neural networks
\theta=(\theta^{1},\dotsc,\theta^{i},\dotsc,\theta^{K})
are updated at the end of every such step according to

\displaystyle\theta_{n+1}^{i}=\theta_{n}^{i}-\varsigma\frac{1}{\sqrt{\hat{H}^{i}_{n}}+\varrho}\hat{G}^{i}_{n},

where
\varsigma
is a learning rate,
\hat{G}_{n}
and
\hat{H}_{n}
are moving average estimates of the first and second moments of the gradient, respectively, and
\varrho<<1
is a constant.

We use the batches to compute the gradient of a loss function
\hat{L}(\theta,D)
, such as for (
2.3.7
):

\displaystyle g_{n}=\frac{1}{m}\nabla_{\theta}L\left(\theta,D_{e}^{(nm:nm+m)}\right),

(A.6.1)

where
D_{e}^{(nm:nm+m)}
denotes the data segment in the
n
th batch of size
m
at epoch
e
.

The moving averages
G_{0}
and
H_{0}
are initialized as vectors of zeros at the first epoch. Then the Adam method updates the moving average estimates and includes bias corrections to account for their initialization at the origin:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}G_{n}=\beta_{1}G_{n-1}+(1-\beta_{1})g_{n-1},\\
\hat{G}_{n}=\frac{G_{n}}{1-\beta_{1}^{n}},\end{cases}

where
\beta_{1}>0
, with
\beta_{1}^{n}
denoting
\beta_{1}
to the power
n
.

Similarly, we compute the second moments by

\displaystyle\begin{cases}H_{n}=\beta_{2}H_{n-1}+(1-\beta_{2})g_{n-1}^{2},\\
\hat{H}_{n}=\frac{H_{n}}{1-\beta_{2}^{n}},\end{cases}

where
g_{n}^{2}
means the elementwise square,
\beta_{2}>0
with
\beta_{2}^{n}
denoting
\beta_{2}
to the power
n
.

Each subsequent epoch continues the count over
n
and keeps updating the moving average estimates
G_{n},H_{n}
starting from their final values of the latest epoch.

Table
A.6
and Table
A.6
list the PPO hyperparameters we choose for the experiments in Section
2.4
. Table
A.6
reports the estimates of the running time of Algorithm
2.3.3
.

In the Algorithm
2.3.3
we use finite length episodes to estimate the expectation of the loss function in line 10. For each episode we need to specify an initial state.
We propose sampling the initial states from the set of states generated during previous policy iterations.
We consider the
i
th policy iteration of the algorithm. We need to choose initial states to simulate policy
\pi_{i}
. Since policy
\pi_{i-1}
has been simulated in the
(i-1)
th iteration of the algorithm, we can sample
Q
states uniformly at random from the episodes generated under policy
\pi_{i-1}
and save them in memory. Then we use them as initial states for
Q
episodes under
\pi_{i}
policy. For policy
\pi_{0}
all
Q
episodes start from state
x=(0,\dotsc,0).

Appendix B
Chapter 2 of Appendix

B.1
Proofs of the theorems in Section
3.2.4

Proof of Lemma
9
.

First, we note that due to the martingale property

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\theta}}\Big[

\displaystyle V_{\phi}(x^{(1,1)})-V_{\phi}(x^{(H+1,1)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\Big(\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{P}(x^{(t,I_{t})},a^{(t,I_{t})},y)V_{\phi}(y)-V_{\phi}(x^{(t,I_{t})})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I_{t}-1}\Big(V_{\phi}(x^{(t,i+1)})-V_{\phi}(x^{(t,i)})\Big)\Big)\Big]=0,

see, for example,
[
53
]
.

Then

\displaystyle V_{\theta}(x^{(1,1)})-V_{\phi}(x^{(1,1)})=V_{\theta}(x^{(1,1)})-V_{\phi}(x^{(1,1)})+\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\theta}}\Big[V_{\phi}(x^{(1,1)})-V_{\phi}(x^{(H+1,1)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\Big(\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{P}(x^{(t,I_{t})},a^{(t,I_{t})},y)V_{\phi}(y)-V_{\phi}(x^{(t,I_{t})})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I_{t}-1}\Big(V_{\phi}(x^{(t,i+1)})-V_{\phi}(x^{(t,i)})\Big)\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=V_{\theta}(x^{(1,1)})+\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\theta}}\left[\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\left(\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{P}(x^{(t,I_{t})},a^{(t,I_{t})},y)V_{\phi}(y)-V_{\phi}(x^{(t,I_{t})})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I_{t}-1}\Big(V_{\phi}(x^{(t,i+1)})-V_{\phi}(x^{(t,i)})\Big)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\theta}}\left[\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I_{t}}g(s^{(t,i)},a^{(t,i)})\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\theta}}\left[\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\left(\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{P}(x^{(t,I_{t})},a^{(t,I_{t})},y)V_{\phi}(y)-V_{\phi}(x^{(t,I_{t})})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I_{t}-1}\Big(V_{\phi}(x^{(t,i+1)})-V_{\phi}(x^{(t,i)})\Big)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\theta}}\Big[\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\Big(g(x^{(t,I_{t})},a^{(t,I_{t})})+\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{P}(s^{(t,I_{t})},a^{(t,I_{t})},y)V_{\phi}(y)-V_{\phi}(x^{(t,I_{t})})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I_{t}-1}\Big(g(x^{(t,i)},a^{(t,i)})+V_{\phi}(x^{(t,i+1)})-V_{\phi}(x^{(t,i)})\Big)\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\theta}}\left[\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I_{t}}A_{\phi}(x^{(t,i)},a^{(t,i)})\right].

∎

Proof of Theorem
2
.

We recall that we defined an occupation measure of policy
\pi_{\theta}^{\Sigma}
at epoch
t
as a distribution over states of
\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma}
as

\displaystyle\mu_{\theta}(t,x):=\mathbb{P}(x^{(t)}=x),\quad\text{ for each }t=1,\dotsc,H,~x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma},

where
x^{(t)}
is a state of the MDP at epoch
t
under policy
\pi^{\Sigma}_{\theta}
.
We also defined the probability that starting at state
x^{(t,1)}=x
at epoch
t
the SDM process is at state
y
after
i-1
steps under policy
\pi_{\theta}
as

\displaystyle\xi_{\theta}(t,i,x,y):=\mathbb{P}(x^{(t,i)}=y~|~x^{(t,1)}=x),

for each
t=1,\dotsc,H,

i=1,\dotsc,I_{t},

y\in\mathcal{X},

x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma}
.

First, starting from the result of Lemma
9
we get the policy improvement bound for the original MDP:

\displaystyle V_{\theta}(x^{(1,1)})-V_{\phi}(x^{(1,1)})

\displaystyle\geq\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma}}\mu_{\phi}(t,x)\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}}\pi^{\Sigma}_{\theta}(a|x)A^{\Sigma}_{\phi}(x,a)

(B.1.1)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\max\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma},~a\in\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}}\left|A_{\phi}^{\Sigma}(x,a)\right|\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma}}\left|\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\mu_{\phi}(t,x)-\mu_{\theta}(t,x)\right|.

Indeed,

\displaystyle V_{\theta}(x^{(1,1)})-V_{\phi}(x^{(1,1)})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\theta}}\left[\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I_{t}}A_{\phi}(x^{(t,i)},a^{(t,i)})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\theta}}\left[\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}A_{\phi}^{\Sigma}(x^{(t)},a^{(t)})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma}}\mu_{\theta}(t,x)\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}}\pi^{\Sigma}_{\theta}(a|x)A^{\Sigma}_{\phi}(x,a)

\displaystyle\geq\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma}}\mu_{\phi}(t,x)\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}}\pi^{\Sigma}_{\theta}(a|x)A^{\Sigma}_{\phi}(x,a)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\max\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma},~a\in\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}}\left|A_{\phi}^{\Sigma}(x,a)\right|\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma}}\left|\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\mu_{\phi}(t,x)-\mu_{\theta}(t,x)\right|.

Next, we find the policy improvement bound for the policies that define atomic actions. A sampled composed action
a^{(t)}\sim\pi^{\Sigma}
defines the path of the SDM process. The goal is to replace values related to the original MDP (i.e.
\pi^{\Sigma}
,
A^{\Sigma}
) by the values related to the SDM process (i.e.
\pi
,
A
). First, we reformulate
\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}}\pi^{\Sigma}_{\theta}(a|x)A^{\Sigma}_{\phi}(x,a)
. Although, this expression does not directly depend on
t
, we abuse the notation and use superscript
t
to distinguish states and actions
(x^{(t,i)},a^{(t,i)})
of the SDM process from states and actions of the original MDP.

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{a^{(t)}\in\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}}\pi^{\Sigma}_{\theta}(a|x)A^{\Sigma}_{\phi}(x,a)

\displaystyle=\underset{a\sim\pi_{\theta}^{\Sigma}(\cdot|x)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A_{\phi}^{\Sigma}(x,a)\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{(x^{(t,i)},a^{(t,i)})\sim\pi_{\theta}^{\Sigma}(\cdot|x)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I_{t}}A_{\phi}(x^{(t,i)},a^{(t,i)})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I_{t}}\underset{(x^{(t,i)},a^{(t,i)})\sim\pi_{\theta}^{\Sigma}(\cdot|x^{(t)})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A_{\phi}(x^{(t,i)},a^{(t,i)})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I_{t}}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{(t,i)}\sim\xi_{\theta}(t,i,x,y)\\
a^{(t,i)}\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot|x^{(t,i)})\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A_{\phi}(x^{(t,i)},a^{(t,i)})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I_{t}}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\xi_{\theta}(t,i,x,y)\sum\limits_{a^{(t,i)}\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\theta}(a^{(t,i)}|y)A_{\phi}(y,a^{(t,i)}),

(B.1.2)

where in the second equality we started to use superscript
t
to specify states and actions of the SDM process: action
a
is decomposed as
a=(a^{(t,1)},\dotsc,a^{(t,I_{t})})
, state
x^{(t,i)}
denotes a state of the SDM process after
i-1
steps, the SDM process starts at state
x
.

We combine (
B.1.1
), (
B.1
) and obtain a policy improvement bound for an MDP with a SDM process:

\displaystyle V_{\theta}(x^{(1,1)})-V_{\phi}(x^{(1,1)})

\displaystyle\geq\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma}}\mu_{\phi}(t,x)\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}}\pi^{\Sigma}_{\theta}(a|x)A^{\Sigma}_{\phi}(x,a)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\max\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma},~a\in\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}}\left|A_{\phi}^{\Sigma}(x,a)\right|\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma}}\left|\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\mu_{\phi}(t,x)-\mu_{\theta}(t,x)\right|

\displaystyle\geq\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma}}\mu_{\phi}(t,x)\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I_{t}}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\xi_{\theta}(t,i,x,y)\sum\limits_{a^{(t,i)}\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\theta}(a^{(t,i)}|y)A_{\phi}(y,a^{(t,i)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\max\limits_{s\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma},~a\in\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}}\left|A_{\phi}^{\Sigma}(x,a)\right|\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma}}|\mu_{\phi}(t,x)-\mu_{\theta}(t,x)|

\displaystyle\geq\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma}}\mu_{\phi}(t,x)\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I_{t}}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\xi_{\phi}(t,i,x,y)\sum\limits_{a^{(t,i)}\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\theta}(a^{(t,i)}|y)A_{\phi}(y,a^{(t,i)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\max\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X},a\in\mathcal{A}}|A_{\phi}(x,a)|\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma}}\mu_{\phi}(t,x)\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I_{t}}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}|\xi_{\phi}(t,i,x,y)-\xi_{\theta}(t,i,x,y)|

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\max\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma},~a\in\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}}|A_{\phi}^{\Sigma}(x,a)|\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma}}|\mu_{\phi}(t,x)-\mu_{\theta}(t,x)|

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x^{(t,i)}\sim\pi_{\phi}}\left[\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I_{t}}\frac{\pi_{\theta}(a^{(t,i)}|x^{(t,i)})}{\pi_{\phi}(a^{(t,i)}|x^{(t,i)})}A_{\phi}(x^{(t,i)},a^{(t,i)})\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\max\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X},a\in\mathcal{A}}|A_{\phi}(x,a)|\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma}}\mu_{\phi}(t,x)\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I_{t}}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}|\xi_{\phi}(t,i,x,y)-\xi_{\theta}(t,i,x,y)|

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\max\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma},~a\in\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}}|A_{\phi}^{\Sigma}(x,a)|\sum\limits_{t=1}^{H}\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\Sigma}}|\mu_{\phi}(t,x)-\mu_{\theta}(t,x)|.

∎

B.2
Neural network structure

In this section we focus on the architecture of the policy neural network (NN) used for atomic actions sampling in the SDM process.
The value NN has identical architecture except
the output layer. The
output layer of the value NN contains one unit with a linear activation function.

We use
\theta
to denote the vector of weights and
biases of the neural network.
For a fixed parameter
\theta
, the
neural network outputs deterministically distribution

\pi_{\theta}(\cdot|x)
over the atomic action space (trip types) for each state

x\in\mathcal{X}
.
We consider a ride-hailing transportation network with
R
regions, patience time
L
, and length of a working day
H
.

We start with a description of the input layer.
We recall that each state
x^{(t,i)}
of the SDM process has four components
x^{(t,i)}=\left[x^{(t,i)}_{e},x^{(t,i)}_{c},x^{(t,i)}_{p},x^{(t,i)}_{\ell}\right]
, where the first three components
x^{(t,i)}_{e}
,
x^{(t,i)}_{c}
,
x^{(t,i)}_{p}
represent current epoch, cars status, and passengers status, respectively, and component
x^{(t,i)}_{\ell}
tracks the cars exiting the available cars pool until the next decision epoch. Based on a system state, the policy NN generates a sampling probability distribution over atomic actions. Next, we discuss how we encode each state component as an input to the NN.

Component
x^{(t,i)}_{e}
is a categorical variable that takes integer values in range
1,\dotsc,H
. We use entity embedding, see
[
44
]
, to encode this component as a low-dimensional vector. First, we apply one-hot embedding to represent a value of
x^{(t,i)}_{e}
as a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{H}
. Namely, we map
x^{(t,i)}_{e}=t
into vector
x^{(t)}_{\text{one-hot}}=(0,\dotsc,0,1,0,\dotsc,0)^{T}
, where the
t
-th element of vector
x^{(t)}_{\text{one-hot}}
is equal to
1
and the rest elements are equal to
0
.

We define an
embedding matrix

E
as an
H\times B
matrix that is a transformation from a set of one-hot vectors into a continuous vector space with dimensionality
B
. Matrix
E
maps each one-hot embedded vector
x^{(t)}_{\text{one-hot}}
into a vector
y^{(t)}
of size
B
:

\displaystyle y_{e}^{(t)}=Ex^{(t)}_{\text{one-hot}},

where
y_{e}^{(t)}
is a part of the input vector of the NN.

Elements of matrix
E
are training parameters of the NN and are included in
\theta
. In other words, the embedding matrix
E
is not given but learned during the NN parameters optimization.

Next, the cars status component
x^{(t,i)}_{c}
is represented by a vector that is divided into
R
parts, one for each region. The
r
-th part of
x^{(t,i)}_{c}
contains
\tau^{\text{max}}_{r}+L
elements, where
\tau^{\text{max}}_{r}
is the maximum time (in minutes) that is required for a driver to reach region
r
from any location of the transportation network. The
k
-th element of the
r
-th part of
x^{(t,i)}_{c}
counts the number of cars that have final destination in region
r
and that are
k
minutes away from it.

The passengers status vector
x^{(t,i)}_{p}
has
R^{2}
elements. Each element of
x^{(t,i)}_{p}
corresponds to one of the trip types
(o,d)
and counts the number of passengers that want to get a ride from region
o
to region
d
, where
o,d=1,\dotsc,R
. Component
x^{(t,i)}_{\ell}
, that tracks the cars exiting the available cars pool, is represented by an
R(L+1)
-dimensional vector. Element
(x^{(t,i)}_{\ell})_{r,k}
of this vector counts the number of cars which final destination or current location is region
r
and which are
k
minutes away from their destination, where
r=1,\dotsc,R
,
k=0,1,\dotsc,L
.

After standard normalization, vector
x^{(t,i)}_{\text{input}}=\left[y_{e}^{(t)},x^{(t,i)}_{c},x^{(t,i)}_{p},x^{(t,i)}_{\ell}\right]
is used as an input to the NN. The input layer has
K:=B+\sum\limits_{r=1}^{R}(\tau^{\text{max}}_{r}+L)+R^{2}+R(L+1)
units, one for each element of the input vector
x^{(t,i)}_{\text{input}}
.

The input layer has
K
units, the first hidden layer has
K
units, the third hidden layer has
5
units, where
5
is a fixed number. The number of units in the second hidden layer is a geometric mean of units in the first and third hidden layers (i.e. integer part of
\sqrt{5K}
). The output layer of the policy NN has
R^{2}
units, one for each trip type. The softmax function is used as the activation function in the output layer of the policy neural network. The policy NN is a feed-forward policy NN and its layers are joined accordantly, see Appendix
A.5
for details.

For the nine-region transportation network, the input layer is of size
527
.
It consists of the cars status component
x^{(t,i)}_{c}
(
388
entries), the passengers status component
x^{(t,i)}_{p}
(
81
entries), the “do nothing” cars component
x^{(t,i)}_{\ell}
(
54
entries). The time-of-day component
x^{(t,i)}_{e}
is a categorical variable taking one of
H=240
values and it is additionally processed into an embedding layer
y_{e}^{(t)}
of size
B=4
. The first, second, and third hidden layers are of size
527
,
51
,
5
, respectively. The output layer of the policy NN has
81
units.

B.3

3.3

We use Tensorflow v1.13.1
[
1
]
to build a training routine of the neural networks and Ray package v0.6.6
[
97
]

to maintain parallel simulation of the actors. We run all experiments on a 2.7 GHz 96-core processor with 1510 GB of RAM.

We optimize the value and policy functions to minimize the corresponding loss functions (
2.3.4
), (
2.3.7
) by the Adaptive Moment Estimation (Adam) method
[
70
]
, see the details in Appendix
A.6
.

Appendix C
Chapter 3 of Appendix

C.1
Proofs of the theorems in Section
4.1

Proof of Lemma
10
.

The discounted future state distribution can be expressed in a vector form as:

\displaystyle(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}(\gamma P_{\pi})^{t}

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}(I-\gamma P_{\pi})^{-1}.

(C.1.1)

If
\gamma<1
, matrix
P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}
is irreducible and aperiodic regardless of
P_{\pi}
, see
[
76
]
.

Now, we need to show that
(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}P_{\pi}^{(\gamma)}=(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}
. Indeed, using (
C.1
) and (
4.1.1
), we get

\displaystyle(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}P_{\pi}^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}(\gamma P_{\pi})^{t}P_{\pi}^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}(\gamma P_{\pi})^{t}\Big(\gamma P_{\pi}+(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T}\Big)

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}(\gamma P_{\pi})^{t+1}+(1-\gamma)^{2}\mu^{T}\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}e\mu^{T}

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}(\gamma P_{\pi})^{t+1}+(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\left(\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}(\gamma P_{\pi})^{t+1}+I\right)

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}(\gamma P_{\pi})^{t}

\displaystyle=(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
12
.

The performance difference identity, proposed in
[
22
,
64
]
, allows to express the difference in performance between two policies
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
as

\eta^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}(\mu)-\eta^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}(\mu)=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}\\
a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right].

(C.1.2)

Starting from the performance difference identity (
C.1.2
) we get

\displaystyle\eta^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}(\mu)-\eta^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}\\
a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}\\
a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]+\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}(x)-d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}(x)\right)\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}\\
a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]+\max\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Big[\underset{a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)}{\mathbb{E}}[A^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)]\Big]\left\|d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}-d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}\right\|_{1}.

Then in
[
4
, Lemma 1]
, the following perturbation identity was derived for discounted stationary distributions:

\displaystyle(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}})^{T}-(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}})^{T}=\gamma(d_{\gamma}^{\pi_{1}})^{T}(P_{\pi_{1}}-P_{\pi_{2}})(I-\gamma P_{\pi_{2}})^{-1}.

(C.1.3)

[
4
, Lemma 1]
finalized the proof of
[
4
, Corollary 1]
showing that

\displaystyle\left\|(I-\gamma P_{\pi_{2}})^{-1}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma}

(C.1.4)

and

\displaystyle\left\|(P_{\pi_{1}}-P_{\pi_{2}})^{T}d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}\right\|_{1}\leq 2\underset{x\sim d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\text{TV}\Big(\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)~||~\pi_{1}(\cdot|x)\Big)\right],

which combined result in

\displaystyle\left\|d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{2}}-d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\gamma\left\|\left((I-\gamma P_{\pi_{2}})^{-1}\right)^{T}(P_{\pi_{1}}-P_{\pi_{2}})^{T}d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\gamma}{(1-\gamma)}\underset{x\sim d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\text{TV}\Big(\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)~||~\pi_{1}(\cdot|x)\Big)\right].

∎

Proof of Lemma
13
.

Using the definition of a discounted transition matrix (
4.1.1
), group inverse
D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}
can be written as

\displaystyle D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}+e(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}

\displaystyle=\left(I-P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}+e(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(I-\gamma P_{\pi}-(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T}+e(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}\right)^{-1}.

Then

\displaystyle D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi_{1}}+e(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}

\displaystyle=\left(I-\gamma P_{\pi}-(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T}+e(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(I-\gamma P_{\pi}\right)^{-1}-\frac{\left(I-\gamma P_{\pi}\right)^{-1}(-(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T}+e(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T})\left(I-\gamma P_{\pi}\right)^{-1}}{1+(-(1-\gamma)\mu+d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}(I-\gamma P_{\pi})^{-1}e}

\displaystyle=\left(I-\gamma P_{\pi}\right)^{-1}-\frac{\left(I-\gamma P_{\pi}\right)^{-1}e(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}\left(-I+\left(I-\gamma P_{\pi}\right)^{-1}\right)}{1+(-(1-\gamma)\mu+d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}(I-\gamma P_{\pi})^{-1}e}

\displaystyle=\left(I-\gamma P_{\pi}\right)^{-1}-\frac{e(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}\left(-I+\left(I-\gamma P_{\pi}\right)^{-1}\right)}{1-\gamma+(-(1-\gamma)\mu+d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}e}

\displaystyle=\left(I-\gamma P_{\pi}\right)^{-1}+e(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}\Big(I-\left(I-\gamma P_{\pi}\right)^{-1}\Big),

where the second equality is due to Sherman–Morrison formula
[
43
, Section 2.1.4]
, the third equality follows from (
C.1
), and the fourth equality is held since

(1-\gamma)(I-\gamma P_{\pi})^{-1}e=e.

∎

Proof of Lemma
15
.

First, let us prove that for any
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
the discounted transition matrix
P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}
satisfies

\displaystyle(P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{\ell}

\displaystyle=\left(\gamma P_{\pi}+(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T}\right)^{\ell}\widetilde{\geq}\gamma^{\ell}\left(P_{\pi}\right)^{\ell}+(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T},

(C.1.5)

where matrix inequality
A\widetilde{\geq}B
means
A(x,y)\geq B(x,y)
for each
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
.

We prove inequality (
C.1.5
) by induction. The base case
\ell=1
is obvious. We assume that (
C.1.5
) holds for
\ell-1
. Then

\displaystyle\left(\gamma P_{\pi}+(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T}\right)^{\ell}

\displaystyle=\left(\gamma P_{\pi}+(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T}\right)\left(\gamma P_{\pi}+(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T}\right)^{\ell-1}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\geq}\left(\gamma P_{\pi}+(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T}\right)\left(\gamma^{\ell-1}P_{\pi}^{\ell-1}+(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T}\right)

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\ell}P_{\pi}^{\ell}+\gamma(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T}+(1-\gamma)\gamma^{\ell-1}e\mu^{T}P_{\pi}^{\ell-1}+(1-\gamma)^{2}e\mu^{T}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\ell}P_{\pi}^{\ell}+(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T}+(1-\gamma)\gamma^{\ell-1}e\mu^{T}P_{\pi}^{\ell-1}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\geq}\gamma^{\ell}P_{\pi}^{\ell}+(1-\gamma)e\mu^{T}.

Hence, we have proved (
C.1.5
).

By (
4.1.12
) and (
C.1.5
) the following inequalities hold for
P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}
:

\displaystyle(P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{\ell}(x,y)

\displaystyle\geq\gamma^{\ell}P_{\pi}^{\ell}(x,y)+(1-\gamma)\mu(y)

(C.1.6)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\gamma^{\ell}\delta^{(\mu)}_{\pi}+(1-\gamma)\right)\mu(y),

for each
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
.

We let
d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}
define the stationary distribution of
P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}
. By (
C.1.6
) and
[
114
, Lemma 2]
, we have the following convergence rate bound for transition matrix
P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}
:

\displaystyle\max\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\Big|(P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{t}(x,y)-d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}(y)\Big|\leq 2(\gamma-\gamma^{\ell}\delta^{(\mu)}_{\pi})^{[t/\ell]},

where
[x]
is the greatest integer not exceeding
x
.

We are ready to bound the norm of the group inverse matrix:

\displaystyle\left\|D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}\left((P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{t}-e(D^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}\right)\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}\|(P^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{t}-e(d^{(\gamma)}_{\pi})^{T}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}(\gamma-\gamma^{\ell}\delta^{(\mu)}_{\pi})^{[t/\ell]}

\displaystyle=2\ell\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}(\gamma-\gamma^{\ell}\delta^{(\mu)}_{\pi})^{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\ell}{1-(\gamma-\gamma^{\ell}\delta_{\pi}^{(\mu)})}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
17
.

(a) The fact that
\tau_{1}[Z]=\tau_{1}[D]
follows from (
4.1.5
) and (
4.1.7
). From
[
68
, Theorem 4.4.7]

Z=ee^{T}I_{Z}-MI_{d},

where
I_{Z}
is the diagonal matrix whose elements are diagonal elements of matrix
Z
.

The equality
\tau_{1}[Z]=\tau_{1}[MI_{d}]
follows from (
4.1.7
).

(b) The statement directly follows from definition (
4.1.6
) and (a), see
[
28
, Lemma 4.1(f)]
.

(c) We note that each row of matrix
MI_{d}
sums up to
\kappa
as we noted in (
4.1.14
), see
[
68
, Theorem 4.4.10]
. By
[
120
, equation (6)]

\tau_{1}[MI_{d}]=\kappa-\min\limits_{x,y\in\mathcal{X}}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathcal{X}}d(z)\min[M(x,z),M(y,z)].

Since
d(x),M(x,y)
take nonnegative values for any
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
,

\tau_{1}[D]=\tau_{1}[MI_{d}]\leq\kappa.

A different proof of the fact that
\tau_{1}[D]\leq\kappa
can be found in
[
121
]
.
∎

C.2
Proofs of the theorems in Section
4.2

Proof of Theorem
4
.

Starting from the performance difference identity we get

\displaystyle\eta_{\pi_{2}}-\eta_{\pi_{1}}

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d_{\pi_{2}}\\
a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d_{\pi_{1}}\\
a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]+\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left(d_{\pi_{2}}(x)-d_{\pi_{1}}(x)\right)\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d_{\pi_{1}}\\
a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]+\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left|d_{\pi_{2}}(x)-d_{\pi_{1}}(x)\right|\mathcal{V}(x)\sup\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\frac{\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]}{\mathcal{V}(x)}

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d_{\pi_{1}}\\
a\sim\pi_{1}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{\pi_{2}(a|x)}{\pi_{1}(a|x)}A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]+\left\|\underset{a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)}{\mathbb{E}}[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)]\right\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}\|d_{\pi_{2}}-d_{\pi_{1}}\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}.

Next, we use perturbation identity (
4.1.9
) with
\gamma=1

\displaystyle d_{\pi_{2}}^{T}-d_{\pi_{1}}^{T}=d_{\pi_{1}}^{T}(P_{\pi_{1}}-P_{\pi_{2}})D_{\pi_{2}}.

to get a new perturbation bound:

\displaystyle\|d_{\pi_{2}}-d_{\pi_{1}}\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\left(D_{\pi_{2}}\right)^{T}(P_{\pi_{1}}-P_{\pi_{2}})^{T}d_{\pi_{1}}\right\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\leq\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}\left[D_{\pi_{2}}\right]\left\|(P_{\pi_{1}}-P_{\pi_{2}})^{T}d_{\pi_{1}}\right\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}

(C.2.1)

Ergodicity coefficient
\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}\left[D_{\pi_{2}}\right]
is finite because

\displaystyle\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}\left[D_{\pi_{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}\left[Z_{\pi_{2}}\right]\leq\|Z_{\pi_{2}}\|_{\mathcal{V}}<\infty,

where the first equality follows from (
4.2.4
). Norm
\|Z_{\pi_{2}}\|_{\mathcal{V}}
is finite since
P_{\pi_{2}}
satisfies the drift condition for Lyapunov function
\mathcal{V}
.

We simplify
\left\|(P_{\pi_{1}}-P_{\pi_{2}})^{T}d_{\pi_{1}}\right\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}
term in (
C.2
) to make it more explicit:

\displaystyle\left\|(P_{\pi_{1}}-P_{\pi_{2}})^{T}d_{\pi_{1}}\right\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{V}(y)\left|\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}(P_{\pi_{1}}(x,y)-P_{\pi_{2}}(x,y))d_{\pi_{1}}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{V}(y)\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left|P_{\pi_{1}}(x,y)-P_{\pi_{2}}(x,y)\right|d_{\pi_{1}}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{V}(y)\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left|\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{1}(a|x)P(y|x,a)-\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{2}(a|x)P(y|x,a)\right|d_{\pi_{1}}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{V}(y)\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}P(y|x,a)\left|\pi_{1}(a|x)-\pi_{2}(a|x)\right|d_{\pi_{1}}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left|\pi_{1}(a|x)-\pi_{2}(a|x)\right|d_{\pi_{1}}(x)\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{V}(y)P(y|x,a)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim d_{\pi_{1}}}\left[\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left|\pi_{1}(a|x)-\pi_{2}(a|x)\right|\mathbb{E}_{y\sim P(y|x,a)}\mathcal{V}(y)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim d_{\pi_{1}}}\left[\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{1}(a|x)\left|\frac{\pi_{2}(a|x)}{\pi_{1}(a|x)}-1\right|\mathbb{E}_{y\sim P(\cdot|x,a)}\mathcal{V}(y)\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d_{\pi_{1}}\\
a\sim\pi_{1}(\cdot|x)\\
y\sim P(\cdot|x,a)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left|\frac{\pi_{2}(a|x)}{\pi_{1}(a|x)}-1\right|\mathcal{V}(y)\right].

∎

Lemma 24
.

We consider a
\mathcal{V}
-uniformly ergodic Markov chain with transition matrix
P
on countable state space
\mathcal{X}
that satisfies the drift condition (
4.2.2
). We let
D
be a group inverse matrix of
I-P
, and let
d
be the stationary distribution of
P
.

We consider a nonnegative matrix
\mathcal{T}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(x,y)=P(x,y)-\nu(x)\omega(y)\text{ for each }x,y\in\mathcal{X},

where
\nu:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a nonnegative vector and
\omega:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[0,1]
is a probability distribution such that
\omega^{T}e=1
,
d^{T}\nu>0
,
\nu^{T}\omega>0
.

If there exists constant
\lambda<1
such that

\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\lambda,

then

(a)

the following identity holds

\displaystyle D=ed^{T}\left(\left(d^{T}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{T}^{n}e\right)I-\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{T}^{n}\right)+\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{T}^{n}\left(I-ed^{T}\right);

(C.2.2)

(b)

the following bound holds

\displaystyle\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}[D]\leq\frac{1+\|e\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}\|d\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}}{1-\lambda}.

(C.2.3)

Proof.

Identity (
C.2.2
) was originally proved in
[
65
]
. The proposed formulation of item (a) is similar to
[
79
, Proposition A.2]
.

We show (
C.2.3
) using identity (
C.2.2
) and property (
4.2.4
):

\displaystyle\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}[D]

\displaystyle=\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}\left[\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{T}^{n}\left(I-ed^{T}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{T}^{n}\left(I-ed^{T}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\left\|\mathcal{T}^{n}\left(I-ed^{T}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|I-ed^{T}\right\|_{\mathcal{V}}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\left\|\mathcal{T}\right\|^{n}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1+\|e\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}\|d\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}}{1-\lambda}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
18
.

We define matrix
\mathcal{T}
on state space
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(x,y):=\begin{cases}P(x,y)\quad\text{ if }x\neq x^{*},\\
0,\quad\text{ if }x=x^{*}.\end{cases}

From (
4.2.6
),

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{T}(x,y)\mathcal{V}(y)\leq\begin{cases}\varepsilon\mathcal{V}(x),\text{ if }x\neq x^{*}\\
0,\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Hence,
\|T\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\varepsilon
. Moreover,
\mathcal{T}
is nonnegative and
\mathcal{T}=P-\nu\omega^{T}
, where
\nu(x)=\mathbb{I}_{x=x^{*}}
and
\omega(x)=P(x^{*},x)
for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
Then, from Lemma
24
,

\displaystyle\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}[D]\leq\frac{1+\|e\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}\|d\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}}{1-\varepsilon}.

We multiply both side of (
4.2.6
) by
d
, we get

\displaystyle d^{T}P\mathcal{V}\leq\varepsilon d^{T}\mathcal{V}+bd(x^{*})

Since
d^{T}P=d^{T}
, we get

\displaystyle\|d\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}\leq\frac{bd(x^{*})}{1-\varepsilon}.

∎

Proof of the drift inequality in Example
1
.

We want to confirm that the Bernoulli random walk transition probabilities satisfy the following drift condition with
\mathcal{V}(x)=\rho^{-x/2}
,
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and
C=\{0\}
:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}P(x,y)\mathcal{V}(y)\leq\frac{2\sqrt{\rho}}{1+\rho}\mathcal{V}(x)+\frac{1-\sqrt{\rho}}{1+\rho}I_{x=0}(x),\text{ for each }x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

First, we note that
\mu=1/(1+\rho)
and
\lambda=\rho/(1+\rho)
. Hence,

•

For
x=0
:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}P(x,y)\mathcal{V}(y)-\frac{2\sqrt{\rho}}{1+\rho}\mathcal{V}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\rho}+\frac{\rho}{(1+\rho)\sqrt{\rho}}-\frac{2\sqrt{\rho}}{1+\rho}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\sqrt{\rho}}{1+\rho}.

•

For
x\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}P(x,y)\mathcal{V}(y)-\frac{2\sqrt{\rho}}{1+\rho}\mathcal{V}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\rho}\rho^{-x/2+1/2}+\frac{\rho}{1+\rho}\rho^{-x/2-1/2}-\frac{2\sqrt{\rho}}{1+\rho}\rho^{-x/2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho^{-x/2}}{1+\rho}\left(\sqrt{\rho}+\sqrt{\rho}-2\sqrt{\rho}\right)=0.

∎

Proof of Lemma
19
.

We define a nonnegative matrix
\mathcal{T}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(x,y)=\begin{cases}P(x,y)-P(x^{*},y)\quad\text{ if }x\in C,\\
P(x,y),\quad\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

We note that
\mathcal{T}=P-\nu\omega^{T}
, where
\nu(x)=\mathbb{I}_{C}(x)
and
\omega(y)=P(x^{*},y)
, for each
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
. For each
x\in\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{T}(x,y)\mathcal{V}(y)=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(x,y)\mathcal{V}(y)-\mathbb{I}_{C}(x)\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(x^{*},y)\mathcal{V}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\mathcal{V}(x)+\left(b-\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(x^{*},y)\mathcal{V}(y)\right)\mathbb{I}_{C}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\mathcal{V}(x),

where the first inequality follows from the drift condition (
4.2.2
) and the second inequality follows from (
4.2.9
). Hence,
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\varepsilon
and,
by Lemma
24
,

\displaystyle\tau_{1,\mathcal{V}}[D]\leq\frac{\left(1+\|e\|_{\infty,\mathcal{V}}\|d\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}\right)}{1-\varepsilon}.

We get a bound on
\|d\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}
multiplying both side of (
4.2.2
) by
d
:

\displaystyle d^{T}P\mathcal{V}\leq\varepsilon d^{T}\mathcal{V}+b\sum\limits_{x\in C}d(x)

As a result, we obtain

\displaystyle\|d\|_{1,\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{b}{1-\varepsilon}\sum\limits_{x\in C}d(x).

∎

Proof of Lemma
20
.

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P^{(\gamma)}(x,y)\mathcal{V}(y)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\Big(\gamma P(x,y)+(1-\gamma)\mu(y)\Big)\mathcal{V}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\varepsilon\mathcal{V}(x)+\gamma b\mathbb{I}_{C}(x)+(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\gamma\varepsilon+1)\mathcal{V}(x)+\gamma b\mathbb{I}_{C}(x)-\frac{1-\varepsilon\gamma}{2}\mathcal{V}(x)+(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}(\gamma\varepsilon+1)\mathcal{V}(x)+\gamma b\mathbb{I}_{C}(x)+(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\mathcal{V}\mathbb{I}_{\Omega}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}(\gamma\varepsilon+1)\mathcal{V}(x)+\max[\gamma b,(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\mathcal{V}]\mathbb{I}_{C\cup\Omega}(x),

where
\Omega=\left\{x\in\mathcal{X}:\mathcal{V}(x)<\frac{2(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\mathcal{V}}{1-\gamma\varepsilon}\right\}
.

We note that the first inequality follows from the drift condition (
4.2.2
), the second inequality follows from the fact that

\displaystyle-\frac{1-\varepsilon\gamma}{2}\mathcal{V}(x)+(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\mathcal{V}\leq\begin{cases}0,\quad\text{if }x\not\in\Omega,\text{ i.e. }-\frac{1-\varepsilon\gamma}{2}\mathcal{V}(x)+(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\mathcal{V}\leq 0,\\
(1-\gamma)\mu^{T}\mathcal{V},\quad\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

∎

C.3
Proofs of the theorems in Section
4.3

Proof of Lemma
21
.

We define
\tilde{d}_{\pi_{2}}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[0,1]
as the stationary distribution of the ”generator”
\frac{1}{m_{\pi_{2}}(x)}(P_{\pi_{2}}(y|x)-\mathbb{I}_{x=y})
:

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{\pi_{2}}(x):=\frac{d_{\pi_{2}}(x)m_{\pi_{2}}(x)}{\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}d_{\pi_{2}}(x)m_{\pi_{2}}(x)}=\frac{1}{\overline{m}_{\pi_{2}}}d_{\pi_{2}}(x)m_{\pi_{2}}(x).

Probability distribution
\tilde{d}_{\pi_{2}}
is called the the stationary distribution of ”generator”
\frac{1}{m_{\pi_{2}}(x)}(P_{\pi_{2}}(y|x)-\mathbb{I}_{x=y})
since
\tilde{d}_{\pi_{2}}^{T}e=1
and

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\tilde{d}_{\pi_{2}}(x)\frac{1}{m_{\pi_{2}}(x)}(P_{\pi_{2}}(y|x)-\mathbb{I}_{x=y})=0,\text{ for each }y\in\mathcal{X}.

We start with performance identity
[
23
, equation (20)]
:

\displaystyle\eta_{\pi_{2}}-\eta_{\pi_{1}}=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\tilde{d}_{\pi_{2}}}\Big[\frac{g_{\pi_{2}}(x)}{m_{\pi_{2}}(x)}

\displaystyle-\frac{g_{\pi_{1}}(x)}{m_{\pi_{1}}(x)}+\frac{1}{m_{\pi_{2}}(x)}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}(P_{\pi_{2}}(y|x)-\mathbb{I}_{x=y})h_{\pi_{1}}(y)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{m_{\pi_{1}}(x)}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}(P_{\pi_{1}}(y|x)-\mathbb{I}_{x=y})h_{\pi_{1}}(y)\Big].

(C.3.1)

Starting from (
C.3
) we get the statement

\displaystyle\eta_{\pi_{2}}-\eta_{\pi_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\frac{m_{\pi_{2}}(x)d_{\pi_{2}}(x)}{\overline{m}_{\pi_{2}}}\Big[\frac{g_{\pi_{2}}(x)}{m_{\pi_{2}}(x)}-\frac{g_{\pi_{1}}(x)}{m_{\pi_{1}}(x)}+\frac{1}{m_{\pi_{2}}(x)}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}(P_{\pi_{2}}(y|x)-\mathbb{I}_{x=y})h_{\pi_{1}}(y)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad-\frac{1}{m_{\pi_{1}}(x)}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}(P_{\pi_{1}}(y|x)-\mathbb{I}_{x=y})h_{\pi_{1}}(y)\Big],

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\overline{m}_{\pi_{2}}}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim d_{\pi_{2}}}\Big[g_{\pi_{2}}(x)+\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P_{\pi_{2}}(y|x)h_{\pi_{1}}(y)-h_{\pi_{1}}(x)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad-\frac{m_{\pi_{2}}(x)}{m_{\pi_{1}}(x)}\Big(g_{\pi_{1}}(x)+\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P_{\pi_{1}}(y|x)h_{\pi_{1}}(y)-h_{\pi_{1}}(x)\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\overline{m}_{\pi_{2}}}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim d_{\pi_{2}}}\left[g_{\pi_{2}}(x)+\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P_{\pi_{2}}(y|x)h_{\pi_{1}}(y)-h_{\pi_{1}}(x)-m_{\pi_{2}}(x)\eta_{\pi_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\overline{m}_{\pi_{2}}}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim d_{\pi_{2}}}\left[\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{2}(a|x)\left(g(x,a)+\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{X}}P(y|x,a)h_{\pi_{1}}(y)-h_{\pi_{1}}(x)-m(x,a)\eta_{\pi_{1}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\overline{m}_{\pi_{2}}}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim d_{\pi_{2}},a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)}\left[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right].

∎

Proof of Theorem
5
.

Starting from the performance difference identity from Lemma
21
we get

\displaystyle\eta_{\pi_{2}}-\eta_{\pi_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\overline{m}_{\pi_{2}}}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d_{\pi_{2}}\\
a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\overline{m}_{\pi_{2}}}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d_{\pi_{1}}\\
a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]+\frac{1}{\overline{m}_{\pi_{2}}}\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left(d_{\pi_{2}}(x)-d_{\pi_{1}}(x)\right)\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\overline{m}_{\pi_{2}}}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d_{\pi_{1}}\\
a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]+\frac{1}{\overline{m}_{\pi_{2}}}\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left|d_{\pi_{2}}(x)-d_{\pi_{1}}(x)\right|\max\limits_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\overline{m}_{\pi_{2}}}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim d_{\pi_{1}}\\
a\sim\pi_{1}(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{\pi_{2}(a|x)}{\pi_{1}(a|x)}A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)\right]+\frac{1}{\overline{m}_{\pi_{2}}}\left\|\underset{a\sim\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)}{\mathbb{E}}[A_{\pi_{1}}(x,a)]\right\|_{\infty}\|d_{\pi_{2}}-d_{\pi_{1}}\|_{1}.

Next, we use perturbation identity (
4.1.9
) with
\gamma=1

\displaystyle d_{\pi_{2}}^{T}-d_{\pi_{1}}^{T}=d_{\pi_{1}}^{T}(P_{\pi_{1}}-P_{\pi_{2}})D_{\pi_{2}}.

to get the following perturbation bound:

\displaystyle\|d_{\pi_{2}}-d_{\pi_{1}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\tau_{1}\left[D_{\pi_{2}}\right]\left\|(P_{\pi_{1}}-P_{\pi_{2}})^{T}d_{\pi_{1}}\right\|_{1}.

Following the proof of Lemma
12
we simplify
\left\|(P_{\pi_{1}}-P_{\pi_{2}})^{T}d_{\pi_{1}}\right\|_{1}
term to get

\displaystyle\left\|(P_{\pi_{1}}-P_{\pi_{2}})^{T}d_{\pi_{1}}\right\|_{1}\leq 2\underset{x\sim d_{\pi_{1}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\text{TV}\Big(\pi_{2}(\cdot|x)~||~\pi_{1}(\cdot|x)\Big)\right].

∎
